Rare as the red flower of the hibiscus would be in a florist’s window on Fifth Avenue. Rouge on brown, a warm, insidious chestnut color. But so much more subtle than chestnut. The round face of the first girl, the carnal sympathy of her full, tinted mouth, touched Ray. But something was between them.…
The piano-player had wandered off into some dim, faraway, ancestral source of music. Far, far away from music-hall syncopation and jazz, he was lost in some sensual dream of his own. No tortures, banal shrieks and agonies. *Tum-tum… tum-tum… tum-tum… tum-tum.*… The notes were naked acute alert. Like black youth burning naked in the bush. Love in the deep heart of the jungle.… The sharp spring of a leopard from a leafy limb, the snarl of a jackal, green lizards in amorous play, the flight of a plumed bird, and the sudden laughter of mischievous monkeys in their green homes. *Tum-tum… tum-tum… tum-tum… tum-tum.*… Simple-clear and quivering. Like a primitive dance of war or of love… the marshaling of spears or the sacred frenzy of a phallic celebration.
Black lovers of life caught up in their own free native rhythm, threaded to a remote scarce-remembered past, celebrating the midnight hours in themselves, for themselves, of themselves, in a house in Fifteenth Street, Philadelphia.…
===Chinese===
胭脂给了这些棕肤姑娘一副多么惹眼的、异邦情调的容貌。胭脂这东西，寻常用得那样滥、那样贱，在这里却有了一种罕见的品格。就像扶桑的红花在第五大道一家花商的橱窗里那样稀罕。胭脂敷在棕色皮肤上，是一种温暖的、偷偷媚人的栗色。却又比栗色幽微得多。头一个姑娘那张圆脸，她那丰满的、敷了色的嘴里透出的肉感体贴，打动了雷。可他们中间总隔着点什么。……
弹琴的人已经浪游到音乐某个幽暗、邈远、祖传的源头去了。离杂耍场的切分音和爵士乐远远的、远远的，他迷失在一场肉感的自梦里。没有酷刑，没有平庸的尖叫和哀嚎。*咚-咚……咚-咚……咚-咚……咚-咚。*……音符赤裸、尖利、警醒。像黑肤的青春在莽丛里赤条条地燃烧。爱在密林深处的心窝里。……豹子从绿叶纷披的枝头霍然扑下，豺狗一声嗥叫，绿蜥蜴在求欢嬉戏，一只带翎的鸟儿振翅飞去，淘气的猴子在青翠的家园里蓦地爆出大笑。*咚-咚……咚-咚……咚-咚……咚-咚。*……单纯、清亮、颤动。像一场原始的战争之舞或是爱情之舞……长矛的列阵，或是阳具祭仪那神圣的迷狂。
这些热爱生活的黑人儿女，卷进了自己那自由自在的、天生的节奏里，同一段邈远而依稀难忆的往昔一线牵连，在费城第十五街的一座宅子里，在他们自身之中、为他们自身、出于他们自身，欢庆这午夜的时辰。……

===Original===
“Raided!” A voice screamed. Standing in the rear door, a policeman, white, in full uniform, smilingly contemplated the spectacle. There was a wild scramble for hats and wraps. The old-timers giggled, shrugged, and kept their seats. Madame Laura pushed aside the policeman.
“Keep you’ pants on, all of you and carry on with you’ fun. What’s matter? Scared of a uniform? Pat”—she turned to the policeman—“what you want to throw a scare in the company for? Come on here with you.”
The policeman, twirling his baton, marched to a table and sat down with Madame Laura.
“Geewizard!” Jake sat down, too. “Tell ’em next time not to ring the fire alarm so loud.”
“You said it, honey-stick. There are no cops in Philly going to mess with this girl. Ain’t it the truth, Pat?” Madame Laura twisted the policeman’s ear and bridled.
“I know it’s the Bible trute,” the happy-faced black lady chanted in a sugary voice, setting a bottle of champagne and glasses upon the table and seating herself familiarly beside the policeman.
===Chinese===
“抄场子啦！”一声尖叫。后门口立着一个警察，白人，全副制服，笑眯眯地打量着满场的景象。一阵狂乱，帽子外衣乱抓一气。老资格们吃吃地笑，耸耸肩膀，坐在原处没动。劳拉妈妈把那警察一把推开。
“都把裤腰带扎紧，你们大伙儿接着乐你们的。怎么啦？见了制服就发怵？帕特——她转向那警察——你干嘛要吓唬这一屋子人呀？过来，跟我上这儿来。”
警察转着警棍，迈到一张桌前，挨着劳拉妈妈坐下了。
“乖乖！”杰克也坐下了。“下回告诉他们，拉火警别拉得那么响。”
“可不是嘛，蜜棍儿。费城没有哪个警察敢来招惹你家姑奶奶。是不是这个理儿，帕特？”劳拉妈妈拧住警察的耳朵，扬起脸端起娇态。
“这就是《圣经》一样的真理，”满脸喜气的黑人太太用甜腻腻的嗓音念诵着，把一瓶香槟和几只杯子摆上桌，亲亲热热地在警察身边坐下来。

===Original===
The champagne foamed in the four glasses.
“Whar’s mah li’l chappie?” Jake asked.
“Gone, maybe. Don’t worry,” said Madame Laura. “Drink!”
Four brown hands and one white. Chink!
===Chinese===
香槟在四只杯子里冒着泡沫。
“我那小老伙计呢？”杰克问。
“走了吧，兴许。别操心，”劳拉妈妈说。“喝！”
四只棕色的手，一只白的。叮！

===Original===
“Here’s to you, Pat,” cried Madame Laura. “There’s Irish in me from the male line.” She toasted:

> “Flixy, flaxy, fleasy,
 

 Make it good and easy,
 

 Flix for start and flax for snappy,
 

 Niggers and Irish will always be happy.”
===Chinese===
“敬你，帕特，”劳拉妈妈喊道。“我父系那一头有爱尔兰血统呢。”她举杯念道：

> “菲利克西，弗拉克西，弗利赛，
 

 喝起来要痛快又自在，
 

 菲利开头，弗拉克收梢要爽快，
 

 黑鬼加爱尔兰，永远乐开怀。”

===Original===
The policeman swallowed his champagne at a gulp and got up. “Gotta go now. Time for duty.”
“You treat him nice. Is it for love or protection?” asked Jake.
“He’s loving *her*”—Madame Laura indicated the now coy lady who helped her manage—“but he’s protecting *me*. It’s a long time since I ain’t got no loving inclination for any skin but chocolate. Get me?”

---
===Chinese===
警察把香槟一口灌下肚，站起身来。“这会儿得走了。当值的时辰到了。”
“你待他这么好。这是为了爱呢，还是为了他罩着你？”杰克问。
“他爱的是*她*”——劳拉妈妈朝那位如今忸怩作态、帮她料理的太太努了努嘴——“可他保护的是*我*。好多年好多年了，除了巧克力色的皮肉，我对别的皮肉早没了爱的兴致。懂吗？”

---

===Original===
When Jake returned to the quarters he found Ray sleeping quietly. He did not disturb him. The next morning they walked together to the yards.
“Did the policeman scare you, too, last night?” asked Jake.
“What policeman?”
“Oh, didn’t you see him? There was a policeman theah and somebody hollered ‘Raid!’ scaring everybody. I thought you’d done tuk you’self away from there in quick time becasn a that.”
“No, I left before that, I guess. Didn’t even smell one walking all the way to the quarters in Market Street.”
“Why’d you beat it? One o’ the li’l chippies had a crush on you. Oh, boy! and she was some piece to look at.”
===Chinese===
杰克回到宿舍，见雷睡得安安静静。他没有搅扰他。第二天早上，两人一道朝车场走去。
“昨儿夜里，那警察把你也给吓着了？”杰克问。
“什么警察？”
“哦，你没瞧见他？那儿有个警察，有人扯着嗓子喊‘抄场子啦！’把大伙儿都吓坏了。我还当你早就一溜烟儿从那儿抽了身呢。”
“没有，我猜我走在那前头。一路走回市场街的宿舍，连一个警察味儿也没闻到。”
“那你干嘛要溜？有个小妞叫你给迷上了。嚯，好家伙！那可是个瞧着就够味儿的尤物。”

===Original===
“I know it. She was kind of nice. But she had some nasty perfume on her that turned mah stomach.”
“Youse awful queer, chappie,” Jake commented.
“Why, don’t you ever feel those sensations that just turn you back in on yourself and make you isolated and helpless?”
“Wha’d y’u mean?”
“I mean if sometimes you don’t feel as I felt last night?”
“Lawdy no. Young and pretty is all I feel.”
===Chinese===
“我知道。她倒有几分好。可她身上那种香水忒冲，熏得我直犯恶心。”
“老伙计，你这人真怪，”杰克品评了一句。
“怎么，难道你就从来没有过那种感觉——一下子把你整个人往里一翻，叫你孤零零、没了依靠？”
“你这话是什么意思？”
“我是说，你有没有时候，也像昨儿晚上的我那样感觉？”
“老天爷，没有。我就觉着年轻、标致，别的什么也没有。”

===Original===
They stopped in a saloon. Jake had a small whisky and Ray an eggnog.
“But Madame Laura isn’t young,” resumed Ray.
“Ain’t she?” Jake showed his teeth. “I’d back her against some of the youngest. She’s a wonder, chappie. Her blood’s like good liquor. She gave me a present, too. Looka here.” Jake took from his pocket a lovely slate-colored necktie sprinkled with red dots. Ray felt the fineness of it.
“Ef I had the sweetman disinclination I wouldn’t have to work, chappie,” Jake rocked proudly in his walk. “But tha’s the life of a peewee cutter, says I. Kain’t see it for mine.”
“She was certainly nice to you last night. And the girls were nice, too. It was just like a jolly parlor social.”
“Oh, sure! Them gals not all in the straight business, you know. Some o’ them works and just go there for a good time, a li’l extra stuff.… It ain’t like that nonetall ovah in Europe, chappie. They wouldn’t ’a’ treated you so nice. Them places I sampled ovah there was all straight raw business and no camoflage.”
===Chinese===
他们进了一家酒馆歇脚。杰克要了一小杯威士忌，雷要了一杯蛋酒。
“可劳拉妈妈不年轻了呀，”雷接着说。
“不年轻？”杰克龇出一口白牙。“叫她跟顶年轻的几个过过招，我押她赢。她是个奇女子，老伙计。她那血就像上好的酒一样带劲儿。她还送了我一件礼呢。瞧瞧这个。”杰克从口袋里掏出一条可爱的青灰色领带，洒满了红点点。雷捻了捻，货色真精致。
“要是我有那当小白脸的心思，我就用不着干活了，老伙计，”杰克走着，神气地摇摆着身子。“可照我说，那是小不点儿逞风流的营生。我干不来。”
“昨儿晚上她待你的确好。姑娘们也都和气。简直就是一场快快活活的客厅交谊会。”
“哦，那可不！你要知道，那些姑娘不全是干那正经营生的。有的有自己的活儿干，上那儿不过是图个快活，找点外快添补。……欧洲那边可压根儿不是这么回事，老伙计。他们才不会把你招待得那么周到。我在那边尝过的那些地方，全是赤裸裸的现钱买卖，没有一点遮掩。”

===Original===
“Did you prefer them?”
“Hell, no! I prefer the niggers’ way every time. They does it better.…”
“Wish I could feel the difference as you do, Jakie. I lump all those ladies together, without difference of race.”
“Youse crazy, chappie. You ain’t got no experience about it. There’s all kinds a difference in that theah life. Sometimes it’s the people make the difference and sometimes it’s the place. And as foh them sweet marchants, there’s as much difference between them as you find in any other class a people. There is them slap-up private-apartmant ones, and there is them of the dickty buffet flats; then the low-down speakeasy customers; the cabaret babies, the family-entrance clients, and the street fliers.”
They stopped on a boardwalk. The dining-car stood before them, resting on one of the hundred tracks of the great Philadelphia yards.
“I got a free permit to a nifty apartmant in New York, chappie, and the next Saturday night we lay over together in the big city Ise gwine to show you some real queens. It’s like everything else in life. Depends on you’ luck.”
===Chinese===
“那边那些地方，你倒更喜欢些？”
“见鬼，不！黑人的路数我回回都更中意。他们干这个更高明。……”
“真希望我也能像你这样觉出分别来，杰基。我把那些太太们一锅烩，不分种族。”
“老伙计，你这是发疯。这上头你没有经验。那种营生里头，有各式各样的分别。有时候是人定的分别，有时候是地方定的分别。至于那些卖甜头的买卖人，她们彼此的差别，同你在任何别的阶层的人们里见到的差别一样大。有顶气派的私人公寓里的一路，有讲究人的私酒公寓里的一路；再有下三滥地下酒吧的主顾；卡巴莱的宝贝儿，走家眷入口的客人，还有街头飞的流莺。”
他们在一条板道上停住脚。餐车就卧在眼前，停在费城大车场那上百条股道中的一条上。
“我在纽约弄到了一张顶呱呱公寓的自由出入证，老伙计。下回咱们赶上同一天夜里在大城市待班，我就带你去见识几位货真价实的女王。这跟人生里旁的玩意儿一样。全看你自个儿的运气。”

===Original===
“And you are one lucky dog,” Ray laughed.
Jake grinned: “I’d tell you about a li’l piece o’ sweetness I picked up in a cabaret the first day I landed from ovah the other side. But it’s too late now. We gotta start work.”
“Next time, then,” said Ray.
Jake swung himself up by the rear platform and entered the kitchen. Ray passed round by the other side into the dining-room.
===Chinese===
“你可真是条走运的狗，”雷笑道。
杰克咧嘴一笑：“本想跟你讲讲我打大洋那头回来头一天，在一家卡巴莱搭上的一小口甜头。可这会儿太晚了。咱们得开工了。”
“那就留着下回，”雷说。
杰克攀住车尾的通过台，一纵身上车，进了厨房。雷绕到另一边，走进了餐室。

## XIV Interlude / XIV 插曲

===Original===
Dusk gathered in blue patches over the Black Belt. Lenox Avenue was vivid. The saloons were bright, crowded with drinking men jammed tight around the bars, treating one another and telling the incidents of the day. Longshoremen in overalls with hooks, Pullman porters holding their bags, waiters, elevator boys. Liquor-rich laughter, banana-ripe laughter.…

The pavement was a dim warm bustle. Women hurrying home from day’s work to get dinner ready for husbands who worked at night. On their arms brown bags and black containing a bit of meat, a head of lettuce, butter. Young men who were stagging through life, passing along with brown-paper packages, containing a small steak, a pork chop, to do their own frying.

From out of saloons came the savory smell of corned beef and cabbage, spareribs, Hamburger steaks. Out of little cook-joints wedged in side streets, tripe, pigs’ feet, hogs’ ears and snouts. Out of apartments, steak smothered with onions, liver and bacon, fried chicken.

The composite smell of cooked stuff assaulted Jake’s nostrils. He was hungry. His landlady was late bringing his food. Maybe she was out on Lenox Avenue chewing the rag with some other Ebenezer soul, thought Jake.

Jake was ill. The doctor told him that he would get well very quickly if he remained quietly in bed for a few days.

===Chinese===
暮色在黑人区上空凝成一片片幽蓝。勒诺克斯大道鲜活热闹。酒馆灯火通明，喝酒的男人挤挤挨挨地围着吧台，你请我让，讲着当天发生的种种事情。有穿工装、拎铁钩的码头工人，有抱着提包的普尔曼卧车侍者，有餐馆侍者，有开电梯的少年。酒气熏天的笑声，熟香蕉般甜腻的笑声。……

人行道上一片暖融融、雾蒙蒙的忙乱。下白班的女人急急往家赶，好给上夜班的丈夫备晚饭。臂弯里挎着棕色和黑色的纸袋，装着一点肉、一颗莴苣、一块黄油。打光棍过日子的年轻人拎着牛皮纸小包走过，里头是一小块牛排、一只猪排，回去自己下锅煎。

酒馆里飘出咸牛肉煮卷心菜、排骨、汉堡牛排的喷香。横街窄巷里挤着的小吃铺子，飘出牛肚、猪蹄、猪耳朵和猪拱嘴的味儿。公寓楼里飘出洋葱焖牛排、猪肝煎咸肉、炸鸡。

五味杂陈的饭菜味直冲杰克的鼻子。他饿了。房东太太送饭来晚了。八成是在勒诺克斯大道上跟哪个埃比尼泽（Ebenezer）教会的老姐妹嚼舌头呢，杰克想。

杰克病了。医生告诉他，只要安安静静地在床上躺几天，很快就会好。

===Original===
“And you mustn’t drink till you are better. It’s bad for you,” the doctor warned him.

But Jake had his landlady bring him from two to four pails of beer every day. “I must drink some’n,” he reasoned, “and beer can’t make me no harm. It’s light.”

When Ray went to see him, Jake laughed at his serious mien.

“Tha’s life, chappie. I goes way ovah yonder and wander and fools around and I hed no mind about nothing. Then I come back to mah own home town and, oh, you snakebite! When I was in the army, chappie, they useter give us all sorts o’ lechers about canshankerous nights and prophet-lactic days, but I nevah pay them no mind. Them things foh edjucated guys like you who lives in you’ head.”

===Chinese===
“还有，病好之前千万不能喝酒。酒对你的病没好处。”医生告诫他。

可杰克让房东太太每天给他拎两桶到四桶啤酒来。“总得喝点什么吧，”他有他的道理，“啤酒又伤不着我。它是淡的。”

雷来看他，杰克取笑他那一脸正经的模样。

“日子就这么回事，老伙计。我跑到老远老远的地方去，四处浪荡，瞎胡混，什么都不往心里去。后来回到我自个儿的家乡，嘿，真像叫蛇咬了一口！当年在部队，老伙计，他们总给我们念叨各式各样的大道理，什么长疮害病的黑夜啦、防身保命的白昼啦，可我从来没往心里去。那些玩意儿是给你们这种读书人预备的——人整个儿住在脑袋瓜里。”

===Original===
“They are for you, too,” Ray said. “This is a new age with new methods of living. You can’t just go on like a crazy ram goat as if you were living in the Middle Ages.”

“Middle Ages! I ain’t seen them yet and don’t nevah wanta. All them things you talk about am killjoy things, chappie. The trute is they make me feel shame.”

Ray laughed until tears trickled down his cheeks.… He visualized Jake being ashamed and laughed again.

“Sure,” said Jake. “I’d feel ashame’ ef a chippie—No, chappie, them stuff is foh you book fellahs. I runs around all right, but Ise lak a sailor that don’t know nothing about using a compass, but him always hits a safe port.”

“You didn’t this time, though, Jakie. Those devices that you despise are really for you rather than for me or people like me, who don’t live your kind of free life. If you, and the whole strong race of workingman who live freely like you, don’t pay some attention to them, then you’ll all wither away and rot like weeds.”

===Chinese===
“那些道理对你也一样管用，”雷说。“这是个新时代，有新的活法。你不能像一头发了疯的公山羊那样横冲直撞，仿佛还活在中世纪似的。”

“中世纪！我还没见过它们呢，也永远不想见。你讲的那些全是扫兴玩意儿，老伙计。说实话，它们叫我害臊。”

雷笑得眼泪都顺着腮帮子淌下来了。……他想象了一下杰克害臊的样子，又笑了一阵。

“可不，”杰克说。“要是有个小妞，我就得臊——不对，老伙计，那些玩意儿是给你们啃书本的家伙预备的。我照样到处跑，可我就像个压根不识罗盘的水手，却总能撞进一个安稳的港口。”

“不过这一回你没能进港，小杰克。你瞧不上眼的那些防护法子，其实更是为你们预备的，而不是为我、也不是为我这类人——我们过的日子不像你那样自由。要是你，还有整个像你这样自由自在过活的劳工壮汉族群，不肯在这上头留点神，那你们统统会像野草一样枯掉烂掉。”

===Original===
“Let us pray!” said Jake.

“*That* I don’t believe in.”

“Awright, then, chappie.”

---

===Chinese===
“那咱们来祷告吧！”杰克说。

“那玩意儿，我可不信。”

“那好，老伙计。”

---

===Original===
On the next trip, the dining-car was shifted off its scheduled run and returned to New York on the second day, late at night. It was ordered out again early the next day. Ray could not get round to see Jake, so he telephoned his girl and asked her to go.

Agatha had heard much of Ray’s best friend, but she had never met him. Men working on a train have something of the spirit of men working on a ship. They are, perforce, bound together in comradeship of a sort in that close atmosphere. In the stopover cities they go about in pairs or groups. But the camaraderie breaks up on the platform in New York as soon as the dining-car returns there. Every man goes his own way unknown to his comrades. Wife or sweetheart or some other magnet of the great magic city draws each off separately.

Agatha was a rich-brown girl, with soft amorous eyes. She worked as assistant in a beauty parlor of the Belt. She was a Baltimore girl and had been living in New York for two years. Ray had met her the year before at a basketball match and dance.

She went to see Jake in the afternoon. He was sitting in a Morris chair, reading the Negro newspaper, *The Amsterdam News*, with a pail of beer beside him, when Agatha rapped on the door.

Jake thought it was the landlady. He was thrown off his balance by the straight, beautiful girl who entered the room and quietly closed the door behind her.

“Oh, keep your seat, please,” she begged him. “I’ll sit there,” she indicated a brown chair by the cherrywood chiffonier.

===Chinese===
下一趟出车，餐车被调离了既定班次，第二天深夜才回到纽约。第三天一早又接令出车。雷没法抽身去看杰克，便打电话给他那位姑娘阿加莎（Agatha），请她去一趟。

阿加莎常听雷讲起他这位顶要好的朋友，却从来没有见过面。在火车上讨生活的人，有几分在轮船上讨生活的人的那股精神。在那逼仄的小天地里，他们不得不结成某种伙伴情谊。在沿途停留的城市里，他们成双成对、三五成群地闲逛。可餐车一回到纽约，这份情谊就在站台上散了伙。人人各走各的路，同伴之间谁也不认识谁。妻子、情人，或是这座大魔城里别的什么磁石，把每一个人各自吸走。

阿加莎是个深棕色皮肤的姑娘，眼睛温柔多情。她在黑人区一家美容院里当助手。她是巴尔的摩姑娘，来纽约已经住了两年。雷是前一年在一场篮球赛加舞会上认识她的。

她下午去看了杰克。阿加莎敲门的时候，杰克正坐在一把莫里斯安乐椅里，读着黑人报纸《阿姆斯特丹新闻》（The Amsterdam News），身边搁着一桶啤酒。

杰克还当是房东太太。进来的却是一位眉目端丽、模样标致的姑娘，随手轻轻把门带上，一下子搅得他乱了方寸。

“哦，您请坐，请坐，”她央求道。“我坐那儿。”她指了指樱桃木五斗柜旁的一把棕色椅子。

===Original===
“Ray asked me to come. He was doubled out this morning and couldn’t get around to see you. I brought these for you.”

She put a paper bag of oranges on the table. “Where shall I put these?” She showed him a charming little bouquet of violets. Jake’s drinking-glass was on the floor, half full, beside the pail of beer.

“It’s all right, here!” On the chipped, mildew-white washstand there was another glass with a toothbrush. She took the toothbrush out, poured some water in the glass, put the violets in, and set it on the chiffonier.

“There!” she said.

Jake thanked her. He was diffident. She was so different a girl from the many he had known. She was certainly one of those that Miss Curdy would have sneered at. She was so full of simple self-assurance and charm. Mah little sister down home in Petersburg, he thought, might have turned out something lak this ef she’d ’a’ had a chance to talk English like in books and wear class-top clothes. Nine years sence I quite home. She must be quite a li’l woman now herself.

Jake loved women’s pretty clothes. The plain nigger-brown coat Agatha wore, unbuttoned, showed a fresh peach-colored frock. He asked after Ray.

===Chinese===
“雷让我来的。他今天一早被派了连班出车，没能过来看你。这个是带给你的。”

她把一纸袋橙子放在桌上。“这些搁哪儿好？”她给他看一小束讨人喜欢的紫罗兰。杰克的酒杯搁在地板上，半满，挨着那桶啤酒。

“行，搁这儿！”那缺了瓷、白漆泛着霉斑的盥洗架上还有一只玻璃杯，插着把牙刷。她把牙刷取出来，往杯里倒了些水，把紫罗兰插进去，摆到五斗柜上。

“好了！”她说。

杰克向她道谢。他有些局促。同他认识的许多女人相比，这姑娘全然是另一种人。柯迪小姐那种人要嗤笑的姑娘里头，她准算一个。她浑身透着朴素的自信和风韵。我老家彼得斯堡（Petersburg）的小妹妹，他想，要是当初有机会说说书上那样讲究的英语、穿穿上等体面的衣裳，兴许也能出落成这样。离家九年了。她如今想必也出息成个小女人了。

杰克喜欢女人的漂亮衣裳。阿加莎那件素净的黑褐色大衣敞着扣，露出里面一件鲜嫩的桃红色连衣裙。他问起雷的近况。

===Original===
“I didn’t see him myself this trip,” she said. “He telephoned me about you.”

Jake praised Ray as his best pal.

“He’s a good boy,” she agreed. She asked Jake about the railroad, “It must be lots of fun to ride from one town to the other like that. I’d love it, for I love to travel. But Ray hates it.”

“It ain’t so much fun when youse working,” replied Jake.

“I guess you’re right. But there’s something marvelous about meeting people for a little while and serving them and never seeing them again. It’s romantic. You don’t have that awful personal everyday contact that domestic workers have to get along with. If I was a man and had to be in service, I wouldn’t want better than the railroad.”

“Some’n to that, yes,” agreed Jake.… “But it ain’t all peaches, neither, when all them passengers rush you like a herd of hungry swine.”

===Chinese===
“这一趟我自己没见着他，”她说。“他打电话跟我讲了你的事。”

杰克夸雷是他顶要好的伙伴。

“他是个好孩子，”她附和道。她问起杰克铁路上的事：“那样从一个镇子跑到另一个镇子，一定很好玩吧。换成我准喜欢，因为我爱旅行。可雷恨透了。”

“自己得干活的时候，就没那么大意思喽，”杰克答道。

“我想你说得对。可跟人短短见上一面，伺候伺候他们，从此再不相见，这里头有种说不出的妙处。它很浪漫。你用不着受家里帮佣那种天天同主人贴身相处的罪。我要是个男人、非帮佣不可，那再没有比铁路更好的差事了。”

“有那么点道理，是的，”杰克附和。……“可这活儿也不全是甜桃儿——那帮乘客跟一群饿急了的猪似的朝你扑上来的时候，就不是了。”

===Original===
Agatha stayed twenty minutes.

“I wish you better soon,” she said, bidding Jake goodbye. “It was nice to know you. Ray will surely come to see you when he gets back this time.”

Jake drank a glass of beer and eased his back, full length on the little bed.

“She is sure some wonderful brown,” he mused. “Now I sure does understand why Ray is so scornful of them easy ones.” He gazed at the gray door. It seemed a shining panel of gold through which a radiant vision had passed.

“She sure does like that theah Ray an unconscionable lot. I could see the love stuff shining in them mahvelous eyes of hers when I talked about him. I s’pose it’s killing sweet to have some’n loving you up thataway. Some’n real fond o’ you for you own self lak, lak—jest, lak how mah mammy useter love pa and do everything foh him bafore he done took and died off without giving no notice.…”

His thoughts wandered away back to his mysterious little brown of the Baltimore. She was not elegant and educated, but she was nice. Maybe if he found her again—it would be better than just running wild around like that! Thinking honestly about it, after all, he was never satisfied, flopping here and sleeping there. It gave him a little cocky pleasure to brag of his conquests to the fellows around the bar. But after all the swilling and boasting, it would be a thousand times nicer to have a little brown woman of his own to whom he could go home and be his simple self with. Lay his curly head between her brown breasts and be fondled and be the spoiled child that every man loves sometimes to be when he is all alone with a woman. *That* he could never be with the Madame Lauras. They expected him always to be the prancing he-man. Maybe it was the lack of a steady girl that kept him running crazy around. Boozing and poking and rooting around, jolly enough all right, but not altogether contented.

===Chinese===
阿加莎坐了二十分钟。

“愿你早日好起来，”她向杰克道别时说。“认识你真高兴。雷这一趟回来，准会来看你的。”

杰克喝了杯啤酒，直挺挺地在小床上躺下，舒展了脊背。

“她可真是一等一的棕肤美人，”他暗自琢磨。“这下我真明白了，雷为什么那么瞧不上那些随随便便的娘们。”他凝望着那扇灰门。那门仿佛成了一块金光闪闪的门板，一位光华照人的仙影刚打那里走过。

“她喜欢那个雷，喜欢得没边儿没沿儿。我提到他的时候，她那双妙不可言的眼睛里，分明闪着爱的光。我琢磨着，有个人那样爱你疼你，准是甜得要命。有个人真心疼你这个人本身，就像，就像——就像我娘从前疼我爹那样，什么都为他操持，直到他冷不丁地一声不吭说死就死了。……”

他的思绪悠悠飘回「巴尔的摩」那位神秘的棕肤小妞身上。她不算雅致，也没念过书，可她人好。也许，要是他能再找到她——总比这样野马似的满世界乱跑强！老老实实想一想，说到底，他从来没有满足过，东边倒一夜，西边睡一晚。在吧台边跟弟兄们吹嘘自己的艳福，倒也能捞到一点自鸣得意的小快活。可灌够了、吹够了之后，要是能有一个属于自己的棕肤小女人，可以回家去，在她跟前做回简简单单的自己，那可就好上千倍万倍了。把鬈发的脑袋枕在她的棕色乳房中间，让她爱抚，做一回撒娇的孩子——男人同一个女人独处的时候，总有那么一会儿喜欢当这样的孩子。跟劳拉妈妈那号女人，他永远当不成这个。她们指望他永远是那个昂首阔步的男子汉。也许正因为身边没有一个安稳的姑娘，才害得他疯疯癫癫四处乱跑。喝酒、嫖女人、四处打滚，倒也快活，可到底不完全称心。

===Original===
The landlady did not appear with Jake’s dinner.

“Guess she is somewhere rocking soft with gin,” he thought. “Ise feeling all right enough to go out, anyhow. Guess I’ll drop in at Uncle Doc’s and have a good feed of spareribs. Hm! but the stuff coming out of these heah Harlem kitchens is enough to knock me down. They smell so good.”

He dressed and went out. “Oh, Lenox Avenue, but you look good to me, now. Lawdy! though, how the brown-skin babies am humping it along! Strutting the joy-stuff! Invitation for a shimmy. O Lawdy! Pills and pisen, you gotta turn me loose, quick.”

Billy Biasse was drinking at the bar of Uncle Doc’s when Jake entered.

“Come on, you, and have a drink,” Billy cried. “Which hole in Harlem youse been burying you’self in all this time?”

“Which you figure? There is holes outside of Harlem too, boh,” Jake ordered a beer.

===Chinese===
房东太太没有露面，杰克的晚饭没了影儿。

“猜她是抱着杜松子酒在哪儿摇摇晃晃呢，”他想。“反正我这身子骨觉着够硬朗，能出门了。猜我该溜达进「老杜克」，美美吃一顿排骨。嗬！这几家哈莱姆厨房里飘出来的东西，简直能把人撂倒。香得叫人受不了。”

他穿好衣裳出了门。“哦，勒诺克斯大道，你这会儿瞧着可真顺眼。老天爷！瞧那些棕皮肤的小宝贝儿，一个个扭得多么欢实！迈着欢天喜地的碎步！简直是一封跳希米舞的请帖。老天爷！药丸子、毒药水，你们可得赶紧把我放喽，快着点儿。”

杰克进门的时候，比利·比亚斯正在「老杜克」酒馆的吧台边喝酒。

“来来来，你这家伙，喝一杯，”比利喊道。“这些日子你把自己埋在哈莱姆哪个窟窿里呢？”

“你猜猜？哈莱姆外头也有窟窿呢，兄弟。”杰克要了杯啤酒。

===Original===
“Beer!” exclaimed Billy. “Quit you fooling and take some real liquor, nigger. Ise paying foh it. Order that theah ovah-water liquor you useter be so dippy about. That theah Scotch.”

“I ain’t quite all right, Billy. Gotta go slow on the booze.”

“Whasmat?… Oh, foh Gawd’s sakel Don’t let the li’l beauty break you’ heart. Fix her up with gin.”

“Might as well, and then a royal feed o’ spareribs,” agreed Jake.

He asked for Zeddy.

“Missing sence all the new moon done bless mah luck that you is, too. Last news I heard ’bout him, the gen’man was Yonkers anchored.”

===Chinese===
“啤酒！”比利嚷起来。“别闹着玩了，喝点儿真家伙，黑鬼。我请客。就点那个你从前迷得要命的漂洋过海来的酒。就是那苏格兰威士忌。”

“我身子骨还没全好，比利。酒得慢慢来。”

“怎么话说的？……哦，看在老天爷份上！别叫那小美人儿把你的心搅碎了。拿杜松子酒把她打发掉。”

“也行，回头再美美来一顿排骨，”杰克同意了。

他问起泽迪。

“打新月挂上天就没影儿了。老天爷赏我这好运气——敢情如今连你也没影儿了。末了儿听人说起他，这位爷在扬克斯（Yonkers）抛锚落脚啦。”

===Original===
“And Strawberry Lips?”

“That nigger’s back home in Harlem where he belongs. He done long ago quit that ugly yaller razorback. And you, boh. Who’s providing foh you’ wants sence you done turn Congo Rose down?”

“Been running wild in the paddock of the Pennsy.”

“Oh, boh, you sure did breaks the sweet-loving haht of Congo Rose. One night she stahted to sing ‘You broke mah haht and went away’ and she jest bust out crying theah in the cabaret and couldn’t sing no moh. She hauled harself whimpering out there, and she laid off o’ the Congo foh moh than a week. That li’l goosey boy had to do the strutting all by himse’f.”

“She was hot stuff all right.” Jake laughed richly. “But I had to quit her or she would have made me either a no-’count or a bad nigger.”

Warmed up by meeting an old pal and hearing all the intimate news of the dives, Jake tossed off he knew not how many gins. He told Billy Biasse of the places he had nosed out in Baltimore and Philadelphia. The gossip was good. Jake changed to Scotch and asked for the siphon.

===Chinese===
“那「草莓唇」呢？”

“那黑鬼早回到他该待的哈莱姆老家了。那个又丑又黄皮的瘦婆娘，他早就蹬了。再说你吧，兄弟。自打你回绝了刚果·罗丝，如今是谁照应你的需要呢？”

“一直在宾州铁路（Pennsy）的围场里野跑呢。”

“唉，兄弟，你可真把刚果·罗丝那颗柔情蜜意的心给伤透了。有天夜里她刚要开口唱《你伤了我的心一走了之》，当着满场卡巴莱就放声哭起来，再也没法儿往下唱。她哭哭啼啼地抽身走了出去，一个多礼拜没上「刚果」露面。那只小呆头鹅小子只好自个儿撑场面摆谱去了。”

“她可是个够劲的尤物，错不了。”杰克畅快地笑了。“可我不得不蹬了她，要不她非把我变成个不中用的窝囊废，要不就变成个撒野斗狠的黑鬼。”

遇上老伙计，又听遍了下等乐子窟里的私房新闻，杰克浑身热乎起来，也不知灌下去多少杯杜松子酒。他把在巴尔的摩和费城嗅出来的那些去处，讲给比利·比亚斯听。这番闲话煞是有味儿。杰克换了苏格兰威士忌，又要了苏打水瓶。

===Original===
He had finished the first Scotch and asked for another, when a pain gripped his belly with a wrench that almost tore him apart. Jake groaned and doubled over, staggered into a corner, and crumpled up on the floor. Perspiration stood in beads on his forehead, trickled down his rigid, chiseled features. He heard the word “ambulance” repeated several times. He thought first of his mother. His sister. The little frame house in Petersburg. The backyard of bleached clothes on the line, the large lilac tree and the little forked lot that yielded red tomatoes and green peas in spring.

“No hospital foh me,” he muttered. “Mah room is jest next doh. Take me theah.”

Uncle Doc told his bar man to help Billy Biasse lift Jake.

“Kain you move you’ laigs any at all, boh?” Billy asked.

Jake groaned: “I kain try.”

The men took him home.…

===Chinese===
头一杯威士忌刚喝完，正要再来一杯，一阵疼痛猛地绞住他的肚子，狠狠一拧，几乎把他断成两截。杰克呻吟着躬下腰，踉踉跄跄退到一个角落，缩成一团倒在地板上。汗珠子布满额头，顺着他僵直的、刀刻似的五官直往下淌。他听见“救护车”这个词反复响了几遍。他最先想起的是他娘。他的妹妹。彼得斯堡那栋小木板房。后院里晾衣绳上晒得发白的衣裳，那棵高大的丁香树，还有那一小块犄角地，春天里结红番茄、长青豌豆。

“别送我去什么医院，”他喃喃道。“我的屋就在紧隔壁。送我上那儿去。”

老杜克叫他的吧台伙计帮着比利·比亚斯把杰克抬起来。

“兄弟，你的腿还能动弹一点儿不？”比利问。

杰克呻吟道：“我动弹不得。”

两个人把他抬回了家。……

===Original===
Jake’s landlady had been invited to a fried-chicken feed in the basement lodging of an Ebenezer sister and friend on Fifth Avenue. The sister friend had rented the basement of the old-fashioned house and appropriated the large backyard for her laundry work. She went out and collected soiled linen every Monday. Her wealthiest patrons sent their chauffeurs round with their linen. And the laundress was very proud of white chauffeurs standing their automobiles in front of her humble basement. She noticed with heaving chest that the female residents of the block rubbernecked. Her vocation was very profitable. And it was her pleasure sometimes to invite a sister of her church to dinner.…

The fried chicken, with sweet potatoes, was excellent. Over it the sisters chinned and ginned, recounting all the contemporary scandals of the Negro churches.…

At last Jake’s landlady remembered him and staggered home to prepare his beef broth. But when she took it up to him she found that Jake was out. Returning to the kitchen, she stumbled and broke the white bowl, made a sign with her rabbit foot, and murmured, foggily: “Theah’s sure a cross coming to thisa house. I wonder it’s foh who?”

The bell rang and rang again and again in spite of the notice: Ring once. And when the landlady opened the door and saw Jake supported between two men, she knew that the broken white bowl was for him and that his time was come.

===Chinese===
杰克的房东太太应邀到第五大道一位埃比尼泽教会的姊妹相好家的地下居室去吃炸鸡。那位姊妹相好租下了这栋老式房子的地下室，把宽大的后院占来晾晒她洗衣的活计。每逢星期一，她出门收取脏床单脏衣裳。顶阔气的主顾会派自家的汽车司机把要洗的衣物送来。白人司机把汽车停在她寒酸的地下室门前，这份体面叫洗衣妇得意非凡。她胸口得意地一起一伏，注意到本街区的太太们都伸长了脖子张望。她这行当非常赚钱。时不时请一位教会的姊妹来吃顿饭，也是她的乐事。……

炸鸡配红薯，好极了。姊妹俩一面吃，一面嚼舌头、灌杜松子酒，把黑人教会里眼下那些丑闻数了个遍。……

到头来，杰克的房东太太总算想起了他，摇摇晃晃地回家去给他炖牛肉汤。可她端着汤上楼的时候，发现杰克不在家。回到厨房，她绊了一跤，摔碎了那只白碗，用她的兔子脚护身符画了一道符，迷迷瞪瞪地咕哝道：“这屋子准是有场灾要临头。不知该应到谁头上呢？”

门铃响了，又一遍接一遍地响，全不顾门上贴着的条子：只按一次。房东太太开了门，看见杰克由两个男人架在中间，她心里明白了：那只摔碎的白碗应在他身上，他的时辰到了。

## XV Relapse / XV 旧病复发

===Original===
Billy Biasse telephoned to the doctor, a young chocolate-complexioned man. He was graduate of a Negro medical college in Tennessee and of Columbia University. He was struggling to overcome the prejudices of the black populace against Negro doctors and wedge himself in among the Jewish doctors that prescribed for the Harlem clientele. A clever man, he was trying, through Democratic influence, to get an appointment in one of the New York hospitals. Such an achievement would put him all over the Negro press and get him all the practice and more than he could handle in the Belt.

Ray had sent Jake to him. …

The landlady brought Jake a rum punch. He shook his head. With a premonition of tragedy, she waited for the doctor, standing against the chiffonier, a blue cloth carelessly knotted round her head. …

===Chinese===
比利·比亚斯（Billy Biasse）给医生挂了电话。医生是个年轻小伙子，皮肤巧克力色，先后毕业于田纳西州一所黑人医学院和哥伦比亚大学。他正苦于要扭转黑人民众对黑人医生的偏见，好把自己硬挤进那班给哈莱姆主顾看病的犹太医生中间去。他是个精明人，正试着通过民主党的门路，在纽约一家医院里谋个职位。这样的成就一达成，各家黑人报纸都会登他的名号，黑人区里的生意会全归了他，病人多到他接都接不完。

是雷（Ray）把杰克送到他这儿来的。……

女房东给杰克端来一杯朗姆潘趣酒。他摇了摇头。她怀着大祸临头的预感等候医生，背靠着五斗橱站着，头上随随便便包着一块蓝布。……

===Original===
In the corridor she questioned Billy Biasse about Jake’s seizure.

“All you younger generation in Harlem don’t know no God,” she accused Billy and indicted Young Harlem. “All you know is cabarets and movies and the young gals them exposing them legs a theirs in them jumper frocks.”

“I wouldn’t know ’bout that,” said Billy.

“You all ought to know, though, and think of God Almighty before the trumpet sound and it’s too late foh black sinners. I nevah seen so many trifling and ungodly niggers as there is in this heah Harlem.” She thought of the broken white bowl. “And I done had a warning from heaben.”

===Chinese===
在走廊里，她向比利·比亚斯问起杰克这次发病的情形。

「你们哈莱姆的年轻一代，没一个心里有上帝的，」她数落比利，控诉整个年轻哈莱姆。「你们心里就装着歌舞厅、电影，还有那帮小丫头们，穿着背带裙，成心把两条腿子露在外头招摇。」

「那我可说不上来，」比利说。

「可你们就该知道才是，趁号角还没吹响，趁黑罪人还来得及的时候，想想全能的上帝。俺活了这把年纪，还没见过哪个地界像这哈莱姆，净是些轻浮放肆、不敬上帝的黑鬼。」她想起了那只打碎的白瓷盆。「俺这不，天上已经给了俺一个警告啦。」

===Original===
The doctor arrived. Ordered a hot-water bottle for Jake’s belly and a hot lemon drink. There was no other remedy to help him but what he had been taking.

“You’ve been drinking,” the doctor said.

“Jest a li’l beer,” Jake murmured.

“O Lawdy! though, listen at him!” cried the landlady. “Mister, if he done had a glass, he had a barrel a day. Ain’t I been getting it foh him?”

===Chinese===
医生到了。吩咐给杰克肚子上放一只热水袋，再喝点热柠檬水。除了他一直吃着的那些药，再没有旁的法子能帮他。

「你喝酒了，」医生说。

「就喝了点儿啤酒，」杰克咕哝道。

「哎哟老天爷！你们听听他这套话！」女房东嚷道。「先生，他要沾了一杯，那就是一天灌一桶。替他打酒的差事，不都是俺干的？」

===Original===
“Beer is the worst form of alcohol you could ever take in your state,” said the doctor. “Couldn’t be anything worse. Better you had taken wine.”

Jake growled that he didn’t like wine.

“It’s up to you to get well,” said the doctor. “You have been ill like that before. It’s a simple affair if you will be careful and quiet for a little while. But it’s very dangerous if you are foolish. I know you chaps take those things lightly. But you shouldn’t, for the consequences are very dangerous.”

===Chinese===
「照你眼下这情形，啤酒是你能沾的酒里头最糟的一种，」医生说。「没有比这更糟的了。你宁可喝葡萄酒还好些。」

杰克嘟哝说，葡萄酒他不爱喝。

「能不能好起来，全看你自己，」医生说。「你这病以前也犯过。只要你仔细些、安生些，将养一小阵子，这本来是桩简单事。可你要是犯傻，那就非常危险。我知道你们这帮人把这类事不当回事。可不该这样，后果是非常凶险的。」

---

===Original---
Two days later Ray’s diner returned to New York. It was early afternoon and the crew went over to the yards to get the stock for the next trip. And after stocking up Ray went directly to see Jake.

Jake was getting along all right again. But Ray was alarmed when he heard of his relapse. Indeed, Ray was too easily moved for the world he lived in. The delicate-fibered mechanism of his being responded to sensations that were entirely beyond Jake’s comprehension.

“The doctor done hand me his. The landlady stahted warning me against sin with her mouth stinking with gin. And now mah chappie’s gwina join the gang.” Jake laughed heartily.

===Chinese---
两天以后，雷那趟餐车回到了纽约。才过晌午，班组的人就到车场去给下一趟跑备料。上齐了货，雷便径直去看杰克。

杰克又见好了。可雷一听他旧病复发，心里着实吃惊。说实在的，在他混的这个世界里，雷这个人未免太容易动感情。他那副肌体里纤细的机簧，会对一些杰克全然无从领会的感受起反应。

「医生把他那套告诫也递给了我。女房东嘴里冒着杜松子酒的臭气，倒教训起我来，叫我不沾罪孽。这会儿俺那老伙计又要入他们的伙喽。」杰克放声大笑。

===Original===
“But you must be careful, Jake. You’re too sensible not to know good advice from bad.”

“Oh, sure, chappie, I’ll take care. I don’t wanta be crippled up as the doctor says I might. Mah laigs got many moh miles to run yet, chasing after the sweet stuff o’ life, chappie.”

“Good oh Jake! I know you love life too much to make a fool of yourself like so many of those other fellows. I’ve never knew that this thing was so common until I started working on the railroad. You know the fourth had to lay off this trip.”

“You don’t say!”

===Chinese===
「可你得多加小心，杰克。你这么明白的人，好话坏话总分得清。」

「哦，放心，老伙计，我会当心的。可不想落成医生说的那种残废。我这两条腿还有好多英里要跑呢，还得去追生活的甜头呢，老伙计。」

「好样的，杰克！我知道你太爱生活了，断不会像那帮伙计里好些人那样，把自己糟蹋成个傻瓜。我上铁路干活以前，从来不知道这病这么常见。你晓得吗，四号侍者这一趟不得不歇工了。」

「不会吧！」

===Original===
“Yes, he’s got a mean one. And the second cook on Bowman’s diner he’s been in a chronic way for about three months.”

“But how does he get by the doctor? All them crews is examined every week.”

“Hm! …” Ray glanced carelessly through *The Amsterdam News*. “I saw Madame Laura in Fairmount Park and I told her you were sick. I gave her your address, too.”

“Bumbole! What for?”

“Because she asked me for it. She was sympathetic.”

===Chinese===
「可不，他摊上的是厉害的一档。还有鲍曼餐车那个二厨，拖成慢性都三个月上下了。」

「那他咋能从医生眼皮子底下混过去？班组的人每个礼拜都要检查的呀。」

「嗯！……」雷心不在焉地翻着《阿姆斯特丹新闻报》（*The Amsterdam News*）。「我在费尔蒙特公园见过劳拉妈妈（Madame Laura），我跟她说你病了。你的地址我也给她了。」

「邦波！干吗给她？」

「因为她开口管我要呀。她挺同情你的。」

===Original===
“I never give mah address to them womens, chappie. Bad system that.”

“Why?”

“Because you nevah know when they might bust in on you and staht a roughhouse. Them’s all right, them womens … in their own parlors.”

“I guess you ought to know. I don’t,” said Ray. “Say, why don’t you move out of this dump up to the Forties? There’s a room in the same house I stay in. Cheap. Two flights up, right on the court. Steam heat and everything.”

===Chinese===
「我从来不给那些娘们儿留地址，老伙计。那可不是什么好章程。」

「为啥？」

「因为你摸不准她们啥时候会撞上门来，跟你大闹一场。那些娘们儿……在她们自家的客厅里，倒是蛮好的。」

「这个你该比我懂，我不懂，」雷说。「哎，你干吗不从这个破窝里搬出来，搬到四十几街那头去？我住的那栋房子里正好有间房。便宜。上两段楼梯，窗子正临着楼里的天井。蒸汽暖气，样样都齐全。」

===Original===
“I guess I could stand a new place to lay mah carcass in, all right,” Jake drawled. “Steam heats you say? I’m sure sick o’ this here praying-ma-ma hot air. And the trute is it ain’t nevah much hotter than mah breath.”

“All right. When do you want me to speak to the landlady about the room?”

“This heah very beautiful night, chappie. Mah rent is up tomorrow and I moves. But you got to do me a li’l favor. Go by Billy Biasse this night and tell him to come and git his ole buddy’s suitcase and see him into his new home tomorrow morning.”

===Chinese===
「要说是换个新地方搁我这副老骨头，我看还成，」杰克慢吞吞地说。「你说蒸汽暖气？这搭那祷告婆婆的热风，我可真是受够洋罪了。再说句实话，那玩意儿也从来不见得比我呵出的气热乎多少。」

「成。你要我啥时候去跟那边的房东太太提这间房？」

「就今晚这个美好的晚上吧，老伙计。我的房钱到明天就到期了，我就搬。可你得帮我个小忙。今晚绕到比利·比亚斯那儿去一趟，叫他明儿早上来提他老哥这口箱子，送老哥进新居。」

===Original===
Jake was as happy as a kid. He would be frisking if he could. But Ray was not happy. The sudden upset of affairs in his home country had landed him into the quivering heart of a naked world whose reality was hitherto unimaginable. It was what they called in print and polite conversation “the underworld.” The compound word baffled him, as some English words did sometimes. Why *under*world he could never understand. It was very much upon the surface as were the other divisions of human life. Having its heights and middle and depths and secret places even as they. And the people of this world, waiters, cooks, chauffeurs, sailors, porters, guides, ushers, hod-carriers, factory hands—all touched in a thousand ways the people of the other divisions. They worked over there and slept over here, divided by a street.

Ray had always dreamed of writing words some day. Weaving words to make romance, ah! There were the great books that dominated the bright dreaming and dark brooding days when he was a boy. *Les Misérables*, *Nana*, *Uncle Tom’s Cabin*, *David Copperfield*, *Nicholas Nickleby*, *Oliver Twist*.

From them, by way of free-thought pamphlets, it was only a stride to the great scintillating satirists of the age—Bernard Shaw, Ibsen, Anatole France, and the popular problemist, H. G. Wells. He had lived on that brilliant manna that fell like a flame-fall from those burning stars. Then came the great mass carnage in Europe and the great mass revolution in Russia.

===Chinese===
杰克高兴得像个孩子，要能蹦跶，他早就蹦跶起来了。可雷并不高兴。他祖国的时局骤变，把他一下子抛进了一个赤裸世界的战栗的心脏，那现实是此前无从想象的。也就是印刷品和体面谈吐里所谓的「下层社会」。这个复合词一直叫他纳闷，有些英文词就常常这样。为什么偏叫「下」层社会，他怎么也想不通。它分明就浮在表层，同人类生活的其他分野一个样；有自己的高处、中间和深处，也有自己的隐秘角落，一如它们。而这个世界里的人——侍者、厨子、汽车司机、水手、搬运工、向导、引座员、运砖小工、工厂工人——同另外那几个分野里的人千丝万缕地相接触。他们在那边干活，在这边睡觉，隔开他们的不过是一条街。

雷一直梦想着有朝一日能写点文字。编织字句，织出传奇，啊！撑起他少年时那些亮堂的梦幻与阴郁的沉思的，正是那些大书：《悲惨世界》《娜娜》《汤姆叔叔的小屋》《大卫·科波菲尔》《尼古拉斯·尼克利比》《雾都孤儿》。

从那些书出发，经由自由思想的小册子，只消一步就跨到了当世那几个才华四射的大讽刺家——萧伯纳、易卜生、阿纳托尔·法朗士，还有风行一时的问题小说家 H. G. 威尔斯。他一直靠那从燃烧的星辰上如火焰倾泻般落下的光辉吗哪活过来。随后，欧洲来了大规模的集体屠戮，俄国来了大规模的集体革命。

===Original===
Ray was not prophetic-minded enough to define the total evil that the one had wrought nor the ultimate splendor of the other. But, in spite of the general tumults and threats, the perfectly-organized national rages, the ineffectual patching of broken, and hectic rebuilding of shattered, things, he had perception enough to realize that he had lived over the end of an era.

And also he realized that his spiritual masters had not crossed with him into the new. He felt alone, hurt, neglected, cheated, almost naked. But he was a savage, even though he was a sensitive one, and did not mind nakedness. What had happened? Had they refused to come or had he left them behind? Something had happened. But it was not desertion nor young insurgency. It was death. Even as the last scion of a famous line prances out this day and dies and is set aside with his ancestors in their cold whited sepulcher, so had his masters marched with flags and banners flying all their wonderful, trenchant, critical, satirical, mind-sharpening, pity-evoking, constructive ideas of ultimate social righteousness, into the vast international cemetery of this century.

===Chinese===
雷的头脑还没有先知到那般地步，说不出前者酿成的全部罪恶，也说不出后者终极的壮丽。但是，尽管遍地是骚乱与威胁，尽管有组织得尽善尽美的举国狂怒，尽管对破了的东西补而不见效、对碎了的东西狂热重建，他的见识也足以让他明白：他已经活过了一个时代的尽头。

他还明白，他那些精神上的师父并没有同他一道跨进新时代。他感到孤独、受伤、被冷落、被欺哄，几乎赤身裸体。可他是个野人，尽管是个敏感的野人，并不在乎赤身裸体。出了什么事？是他们不肯来，还是他把他们丢在了后头？总归是出了事。但那不是背弃，也不是年轻一辈的造反。那是死亡。正如某一名门望族的末代子孙，今天还在腾跃招摇，旋即死去，被安放进先人那白灰粉饰的冰冷坟穴里去；他的那些师父也正是这样，旌旗猎猎，带着他们那一切精妙的、犀利的、批判的、讽刺的、磨砺心智的、唤起悲悯的、建设的、关于终极社会公义的观念，开进了本世纪那片浩瀚的国际公墓。

===Original===
Dreams of patterns of words achieving form. What would he ever do with the words he had acquired? Were they adequate to tell the thoughts he felt, describe the impressions that reached him vividly? What were men making of words now? During the war he had been startled by James Joyce in *The Little Review*. Sherwood Anderson had reached him with *Winesburg, Ohio*. He had read, fascinated, all that D. H. Lawrence published. And wondered if there was not a great Lawrence reservoir of words too terrible and too terrifying for nice printing. Henri Barbusse’s *Le Feu* burnt like a flame in his memory. Ray loved the book because it was such a grand anti-romantic presentation of mind and behavior in that hell-pit of life. And literature, story-telling, had little interest for him now if thought and feeling did not wrestle and sprawl with appetite and dark desire all over the pages.

Dreams of making something with words. What could he make … and fashion? Could he ever create Art? Art, around which vague, incomprehensible words and phrases stormed? What was art, anyway? Was it more than a clear-cut presentation of a vivid impression of life? Only the Russians of the late era seemed to stand up like giants in the new. Gogol, Dostoevsky, Tolstoy, Chekhov, Turgenev. When he read them now he thought: Here were elements that the grand carnage swept over and touched not. The soil of life saved their roots from the fire. They were so saturated, so deep-down rooted in it.

===Chinese===
梦想着字句的图案获得形体。他学下的这些字句，终究能拿来做什么？够不够道出他心里感到的思想，描画出生动传到他身上的印象？如今人们拿字句在做着什么？大战期间，《小评论》（*The Little Review*）上的詹姆斯·乔伊斯叫他吃了一惊。舍伍德·安德森的《俄亥俄州的温斯堡》（*Winesburg, Ohio*）抵达了他。D. H. 劳伦斯发表过的东西，他读了个遍，读得入迷。还猜想劳伦斯是不是另有一座大水库，蓄着怕人、骇人的字句，登不得体面的印刷。亨利·巴比塞的《火线》（*Le Feu*）像一团火在他记忆里烧着。雷爱那本书，因为它把那个生活地狱之坑里的心思与行状作了一次宏大的反浪漫的呈现。至于文学、讲故事，如今要是思想和感情不同食欲和幽暗的欲望在字里行间摔跤打滚，他就不大有兴趣了。

梦想着用字句造出点什么东西。他能造出什么……塑出什么？他到底能不能创造艺术？艺术——围着它打转的，尽是些含糊得叫人发懵的字眼和短语？艺术到底是个啥？除了把一幅对生活的鲜活印象剪裁分明地呈现出来，它还是别的什么吗？似乎唯有上一个时代的那些俄国人，在新世纪里巨人般立着。果戈里、陀思妥耶夫斯基、托尔斯泰、契诃夫、屠格涅夫。如今读他们，他想：这才是那场大屠戮扫荡而过却未曾触动的元素。生活的泥土护住了他们的根，没让火烧着。他们在这泥土里浸得那样透，根扎得那样深。

===Original===
Thank God and Uncle Sam that the old dreams were shattered. Nevertheless, he still felt more than ever the utter blinding nakedness and violent coloring of life. But what of it? Could he create out of the fertile reality around him? Of Jake nosing through life, a handsome hound, quick to snap up any tempting morsel of poisoned meat thrown carelessly on the pavement? Of a work pal he had visited in the venereal ward of Bellevue, where youths lolled sadly about? And the misery that overwhelmed him there, until life appeared like one big disease and the world a vast hospital?

===Chinese===
感谢上帝和山姆大叔，旧梦终究是碎了。尽管如此，他比以往任何时候都更痛切地感到生活那彻底的、炫目的赤裸和暴烈的色彩。可那又怎样？他能就着周围这片丰饶的现实进行创造吗？写杰克——一只漂亮的猎狗，鼻子拱着生活过活，随便什么丢在人行道上的、下过毒的诱人肉屑，它都一口叼起来？写他探望过的一个工友——在贝尔维尤医院（Bellevue）的性病病房里，年轻人凄凄惶惶地东倒西歪？还有在那儿把他淹没的悲惨，直到生活看着像一整场大病，世界像一座巨大的医院？

## XVI A Practical Prank / XVI 恶作剧

===Original===
> My Dear Honey-Stick
> “I was riding in Fairmount Park one afternoon, just taking the air as usual, when I saw your proper-speaking friend with a mess of books. He told me you were sick and I was so mortified for I am giving a big evening soon and was all set on fixing it on a night when you would certain sure be laying over in Philadelphia. Because you are such good company I may as well say how much you are appreciated here. I guess I’ll put it off till you are okay again, for as I am putting my hand in my own pocket to give all of my friends and wellwishers a dandy time it won’t be no fun for *me* if I leave out the *principal* one. Guess who!
> “I am expecting to come to New York soon on shopping bent. You know all us weak women who can afford it have got the Fifth Avenue fever, my dear. If I come I’ll sure look you up, you can bank on it.
> “Bye, bye, honey-stick and be good and quiet and better yourself soon. Philadelphia is lonesome without you.
> “Lovingly,

**Laura**.”
===Chinese===
> 我亲爱的甜棍儿：
> 「一天下午，我正坐车兜费尔芒特公园（Fairmount Park），照平常一样透透空气，一眼瞧见你那位说话文绉绉的朋友，抱着一摞书。他告诉我你病了，我心里难受极了，因为我眼看要办一场大夜会，本来铁了心要挑一个你准保在费城停靠过夜的晚上。你这个人在一起多么叫人痛快，我不妨直说，你在这边多么受抬举。我琢磨还是推到你利索了再说，因为这一回掏的是我自己的腰包，要请所有的朋友和捧场的朋友们痛痛快快玩一场，要是单单落下*顶要紧*的那一位，那对*我*还有什么乐子。猜猜是谁！
> 「我打算不日到纽约采买些东西。你知道，我们这些花得起钱的软性子女人家，都得了第五大道热症啦，亲爱的。我要是来，保管来寻你，你就把心放肚子里吧。
> 「再会，再会，甜棍儿，乖乖地安安静静，早点儿好起来。费城没有你，冷清得很。
> 「爱你的，

**劳拉**。」
===Original===
Billy Biasse, calling by Jake’s former lodging, found this letter for him, lying there among a pile of others, on the little black round table in the hall.…
“Here you is, boh. Whether youse well or sick, them’s after you.”
“Is they? Lemme see. Hm… Philly.”…
“Who is you’ pen-pusher?” asked Billy.
“A queen in Philly. Says she might pay me a visit here. I ain’t send out no invitation foh no womens yet.”
“Is she the goods?”
===Chinese===
比利·比亚斯（Billy Biasse）顺路到杰克先前的住处去，在门厅那张黑色小圆桌上、一堆信件中间，替他找到了这一封。……
「给，兄弟，你的信。不论你好着病着，总有人追着你了。」
「是么？给俺瞧瞧。嗯……费城。」……
「给你摇笔杆儿的是谁呀？」比利问。
「费城的一位女王。说她兴许要上这儿来瞧瞧俺。俺还没给哪个女人发过请帖呢。」
「她够不够好货？」
===Original===
“She’s a wang, boh. Queen o’ Philly, I tell you. And foh me, everything with her is F.O.C. But I don’t want that yaller piece o’ business come nosing after me here in Harlem.”
“She ain’t got to find you, boh. Jest throws her a bad lead.”
“Tha’s the stuff to give ’m. Ain’t you a buddy with a haid on, though?”
“’Deed I is. And all you niggers knows it who done frequent mah place.”
And so Jake, in a prankish mood, replied to Madame Laura on a picture postcard saying he would be well and up soon and be back on the road and on the job again, and he gave Congo Rose’s address.
---
===Chinese===
「她可是一号人物，兄弟。费城的女王，我跟你说。再说在她那儿，俺样样都是白给，不花一个子儿。可俺不想让那件黄皮玩意儿跑到哈莱姆来，追着俺的鼻子转。」
「她找不着你的，兄弟。给她甩个假线索就是了。」
「这才够味儿，就这么治她。你可真是个脑袋顶用的好兄弟，不是么？」
「那还用说。你们这帮常上俺那地方去的黑鬼，哪个不知道。」
于是，杰克存心捣乱，用一张风景明信片回复了劳拉妈妈（Madame Laura），说他很快就会好起来、下床走动，重新上路、重新上工，末了给了她刚果·罗丝（Congo Rose）的地址。
---
===Original===
Madame Laura made her expected trip to New York, traveling “Chair,” as was her custom when she traveled. She wore a mauve dress, vermilion-shot at the throat, and short enough to show the curved plumpness of her legs encased in fine unrumpled rose-tinted stockings. Her modish overcoat was lilac-gray lined with green and a large marine-blue rosette was bunched at the side of her neat gray hat.
In the Fifth Avenue shops she was waited upon as if she were a dark foreign lady of title visiting New York. In the afternoon she took a taxicab to Harlem.
Now all the fashionable people who called at Rose’s house were generally her friends. And so Rose always went herself to let them in. She could look out from her window, one flight up, and ho-ho down to them.
When Madame Laura rang the bell, Rose popped her head out. Nobody I know, she thought, but the attractive woman in expensive clothes piqued her curiosity. Hastily she dabbed her face with powder pad, patted her hair into shape, and descended.
===Chinese===
劳拉妈妈如期踏上了纽约之行，出门照老规矩，坐的是「座椅车」。她穿一条淡紫衣裙，领口闪着朱红的丝光，裙摆短得露出两条腿弯弯的丰腴，裹在挺括不起皱的玫瑰色丝袜里。时髦的大衣是丁香灰的面、衬着绿里子，齐整的灰帽子侧边，簇着一朵海蓝大缎花。
在第五大道的铺子里，人人恭敬地伺候她，仿佛她是一位驾临纽约、有头衔的深肤色外国贵妇。下午，她坐了出租汽车去哈莱姆。
登罗丝家门的时髦人物，一般都是她的朋友。所以罗丝向来亲自下去开门。她从楼上一层的窗口望出去，就能冲下面的人呵呵地喊。
劳拉妈妈按响门铃，罗丝探出头来。哪个都不认得，她想，可这位衣着昂贵、模样动人的女人撩起了她的好奇心。她慌忙拿粉扑往脸上拍了拍，把头发按抚成型，下楼去了。
===Original===
“Is Mr. Jacob Brown living here?” Madame Laura asked.
“Well, he was—I mean—” This luxurious woman demanding Jake tantalized Rose. She still referred to him as her man since his disappearance. No reports of his living with another woman having come to her, she had told her friends that Jake’s mother had come between them.
“He always had a little some’n’ of a mamma’s boy about him, you know.”
Poor Jake. Since he left home, his mother had become for him a loving memory only. When you saw him, talked to him, he stood forth as one of those unique types of humanity who lived alone and were never lonely. You would hardly wonder who were his father and mother and what they were like. He, in his frame and atmosphere, was the Alpha and Omega himself.
===Chinese===
「雅各布·布朗（Jacob Brown）先生住在这儿吗？」劳拉妈妈问。
「这个嘛，他住过——我是说——」这位浑身阔气的女人一开口就要杰克，撩得罗丝心痒难耐。自打他消失以后，她一直管他叫自己的男人。没有一点他同别的女人同居的风声传到她耳朵里，她便对朋友们说，是杰克的娘插进他们中间来了。
「你也知道，他骨子里总带着点儿离不开娘的孩子气。」
可怜的杰克。自从离了家，母亲之于他，只成了一段温爱的记忆。你见到他，同他攀谈，他便挺身立在那里，正是人间那种独一无二、独居而不孤独的人。你几乎不会去稀奇他的父母是何等人、何等模样。他，凭他那副身架与气象，自身便是那阿尔法与俄梅戛——始与终。
===Original===
“I mean—Can you tell me what you want?” asked Rose.
“Must I? I didn’t know he—Why, he wrote to me. Said he was ill. And sent his friend to tell me he was ill. Can’t I see him?”
“Did he write to you from this here address?”
“Why, certainly. I have his card here.” Madame Laura was fumbling in her handbag.
A triumphant smile stole into Rose’s face. Jake had no real home and had to use her address.
===Chinese===
「我是说——你能说说你要什么吗？」罗丝问。
「一定要说么？我不知道他——哎，他写了信给我。说他病了。还打发朋友来告诉我他病了。我不能见见他？」
「他是打这个地址写给你的么？」
「那还用说。他的名片就在这儿呢。」劳拉妈妈在手提包里摸索着。
一丝得胜的笑悄悄爬上罗丝的脸。杰克没有真正的家，只好借用她的地址。
===Original===
“Is you his sister or what?”
“I’m a friend,” Madame Laura said, sharply.
“Well, he’s got a nearve.” Rose jerked herself angrily. “He’s mah man.”
“I didn’t come all the way here to hear that,” said Madame Laura. “I thought he was sick and wanting attention.”
“Ain’t I good enough to give him all the attention required without another woman come chasing after him?”
===Chinese===
「你是他妹妹还是个什么？」
「我是他的朋友。」劳拉妈妈尖声说。
「嗬，他可真有脸。」罗丝气得浑身一挺。「他是俺的男人。」
「我大老远跑来，可不是来听这话的，」劳拉妈妈说。「我只当他病着，正需要人照顾呢。」
「俺照料他还不够周到么，还用得着另一个女人追在他后头跑？」
===Original===
“Disgusting!” cried Madame Laura. “I would think this was a spohting house.”
“Gwan with you before I spit in you’ eye,” cried Rose. “You look like some’n just outa one you’self.”
“You’re no lady,” retorted Madame Laura, and she hurried down the steps.
Rose amplified the story exceedingly in telling it to her friends. “I slapped her face for insulting me,” she said.
===Chinese===
「恶心！」劳拉妈妈嚷道。「我还当这儿是个窑子呢。」
「趁俺没啐进你眼睛里，赶紧滚吧，」罗丝嚷道。「你那副模样，倒像刚打那种窝子里出来的。」
「你可算不上淑女。」劳拉妈妈回敬一句，急急地下了台阶。
罗丝把这件事讲给朋友们听的时候，添油加醋得没了边儿。「她侮辱俺，俺扇了她一个耳光。」她说。
===Original===
Billy Biasse heard of it from the boy dancer of the Congo. When Billy went again to see Jake, one of the patrons of his gaming joint went with him. It was that yellow youth, the same one that had first invited Zeddy over to Gin-head Susy’s place. He was a prince of all the day joints and night holes of the Belt. All the shark players of Dixie Red’s poolroom were proud of losing a game to him, and at the Congo the waiters danced around to catch his orders. For Yaller Prince, so they affectionately called him, was living easy and sweet. Three girls, they said, were engaged in the business of keeping him princely—one chocolate-to-the-bone, one teasing-brown, and one yellow. He was always well dressed in a fine nigger-brown or bottle-green suit, excessively creased, and spats. Also he was happy-going and very generous. But there was something slimy about him.
Yaller Prince had always admired Jake, in the way a common-bred admires a thoroughbred, and hearing from Billy that he was ill, he had brought him fruit, cake, and ice cream and six packets of Camels. Yaller Prince was more intimate with Jake’s world than Billy, who swerved off at a different angle and was always absorbed in the games and winnings of men.
Jake and Yaller had many loose threads to pick up again and follow for a while. Were the gin parties going on still at Susy’s? What had become of Miss Curdy? Yaller didn’t know. He had dropped Myrtle Avenue before Zeddy did.
===Chinese===
这事传到比利·比亚斯耳朵里，是从「刚果」的一个舞男口中。比利再去看杰克的时候，他那个赌场的一位常客也跟了去。就是那个黄皮小伙子——头一个邀泽迪去贪杯苏西家的，正是此人。他是黑人区里一切日间乐子窟、夜间销金窝里的王子。迪克西·雷德（Dixie Red）台球房里所有的高手，都把输他一局引为体面；在「刚果」，堂倌们舞着步子团团转，抢着接他的点单。因为「黄皮王子」（Yaller Prince）——大伙儿都亲热地这样叫他——日子过得又安逸又甜美。据说有三个姑娘，专司把他供养得像王子一般——一个巧克力色透到骨头里，一个撩人的棕色，一个黄皮。他永远衣着体面，一身考究的黑棕色或瓶绿色西服，裤线熨得笔挺，脚上套着鞋罩。人也乐天随和，出手大方。只是他身上总有点儿黏滑的东西。
「黄皮王子」向来仰慕杰克，好比杂牌马仰慕纯种马；听比利说他病了，便送来水果、蛋糕、冰淇淋，还有六包骆驼牌香烟。「黄皮王子」比比利更贴近杰克那个世界；比利拐向了另一个方向，成天沉在男人们的赌局和输赢里。
杰克和「黄皮」有许多松脱的线头要重新拾起，扯上一阵子。苏西家的杜松子酒聚会还办着么？柯迪小姐后来怎么样了？「黄皮」不知道。他撂下默特尔大道，比泽迪还早。
===Original===
“Susy was free with the gin all right, but, gee whizzard! She was sure black and ugly, buddy,” remarked Yaller.
“You said it, boh,” agreed Jake. “They was some pair all right, them two womens. Black and ugly is exactly Susy, and that there other Curdy creachur all streaky yaller and ugly. I couldn’t love them theah kind.”
Yaller uttered a little goat laugh. “I kain’t stand them ugly grannies, either. But sometimes they does pay high, buddy, and when the paying is good, I can always transfer mah mind.”
“I couldn’t foh no price, boh,” said Jake. “Gimme a nice sweet-skin brown. I ain’t got no time foh none o’ you’ ugly hard-hided dames.”
Jake asked for Strawberry Lips. He was living in Harlem again and working longshore. Up in Yonkers Zeddy was endeavoring to overcome his passion for gambling and start housekeeping with a steady home-loving woman. He was beginning to realize that he was not big enough to carry two strong passions, each pulling him in opposite directions. Some day a grandson of his born in Harlem might easily cope with both passions, might even come to sacrifice woman to gambling. But Zeddy himself was too close to the savage swell of life.
===Chinese===
「苏西的杜松子酒倒真是不吝惜，可是，我的老天爷！她实在又黑又丑，兄弟，」「黄皮」说。
「可不，兄弟，」杰克附和。「那两个女人，真真是天造地设的一对。又黑又丑，说的正是苏西；还有那个柯迪怪物，花斑黄皮，也丑。她们那号货色，俺可爱不起来。」
「黄皮」发出一声小山羊似的笑。「俺也受不了那些丑老婆子。可有时候她们舍得大把掏钱，兄弟；只要进账好，俺总能把心思挪挪窝。」
「给多少钱俺也不成，兄弟，」杰克说。「给俺一个皮子甜甜的棕肤俏姑娘。你那些丑得皮硬壳厚的娘们儿，俺可没工夫搭理。」
杰克问起了「草莓唇」（Strawberry Lips）。他如今又住回了哈莱姆，在码头做装卸工。在北边的扬克斯（Yonkers），泽迪正想法子压下他对赌博的那股瘾，要跟一个安稳恋家的女人成家过日子。他渐渐明白过来：自己那副肩膀不够宽，扛不动两股强烈的瘾头——它们各拽各的方向。将来哪个生在哈莱姆的孙儿，兴许能轻巧地两头兼顾，甚至干脆为赌博牺牲女人。可泽迪自己，离生活那股蛮野的浪头太近了。
===Original===
Ray entered with a friend whom he introduced as James Grant. He was also a student working his way through college. But lacking funds to continue, he had left college to find a job. He was fourth waiter on Ray’s diner, succeeding the timid boy from Georgia. As both chairs were in use, Grant sat on the edge of the bed and Ray tipped up Jake’s suitcase.…
Conversation veered off to the railroad.
“I am getting sick of it,” Ray said. “It’s a crazy, clattering, nerve-shattering life. I think I’ll fall down for good.”
“Why, ef you quit, chappie, I’ll nevah go back on that there white man’s sweet chariot,” said Jake.
===Chinese===
雷带着一个朋友进来，介绍说他叫詹姆斯·格兰特（James Grant）。他也是个半工半读的大学生，只因续不上钱，才离了学校出来找活儿。他在雷那趟餐车上接了第四侍者的位子，顶替那个佐治亚来的腼腆小伙子。两把椅子都有人坐，格兰特便坐上床沿，雷把杰克的手提箱立了起来。……
话头岔到了铁路上。
「我快腻味死了，」雷说。「这种日子疯疯癫癫、哐啷乱响，把神经都震碎了。我想我这回要永远撂挑子了。」
「嗨，你要是不干了，老伙计，俺这辈子再不回那白佬的甜蜜战车上去了，」杰克说。
===Original===
“Whasmat?” asked Billy Biasse. “Kain’t you git along on theah without him?”
“It’s a whole lot the matter you can’t understand, Billy. The white folks’ railroad ain’t like Lenox Avenue. You can tell on theah when a pal’s a real pal.”
“I got a pal, I got a gal,” chanted Billy, “heah in mah pocketbook.” He patted his breast pocket.
“Go long from here with you’ lonesome haht, you wolf,” cried Jake.
“Wolf is mah middle name, but… I ain’t bad as I hear, and ain’t you mah buddy, too?” Billy said to Jake. “Git you’self going quick and come on down to mah place, son. The bones am lonesome foh you.”
===Chinese===
「这是说啥？」比利·比亚斯问。「没有他，你们在那上头就处不开了？」
「这里头有一大堆你弄不懂的门道，比利。白佬的铁路，可不像勒诺克斯大道。在那上头，一个伙伴是不是真伙伴，你分得清爽。」
「俺有个伙伴，俺有个姑娘，」比利念经似的哼着，「就在俺这钱包里头。」他拍了拍胸口的衣袋。
「带着你这颗孤孤单单的心上别处去吧，你这匹狼，」杰克笑骂道。
「狼是俺的中间名，不过……俺没人家传的那么坏，再说你不也是俺的兄弟么？」比利对杰克说。「赶紧把身子骨将养利索，上俺那地方来吧，老弟。那副骰子想你都想得寂寞了。」
===Original===
Billy and Yaller Prince left.
“Who is the swell strutter?” Ray’s friend asked.
“Hm!… I knowed him long time in Harlem,” said Jake. “He’s a good guy. Just brought me all them eats and cigarettes.”
“What does he work at?” asked Ray.
“Nothing menial. He’s a p.i.”…
===Chinese===
比利和「黄皮王子」走了。
「那个浑身阔气、走路带响的家伙是谁？」雷的朋友问。
「嗯！……俺在哈莱姆认得他多年了，」杰克说。「人是好人。方才那些吃的抽的，全是他送来的。」
「他干什么活儿？」雷问。
「不下贱的活儿。他是个 p.i.。」……
===Original===
“Low-down yaller swine,” said Ray’s friend, “Harlem is stinking with them.”
“Oh, Yaller is all right, though,” said Jake. “A real good-hearted scout.”
“Good-hearted!” Grant sneered. “A man’s heart is cold dead when he has women doing that for him. How can a man live that way and strut in public, instead of hiding himself underground like a worm?” He turned indignantly to Ray.
===Chinese===
「下贱的黄皮猪猡，」雷的朋友说，「哈莱姆让这号人弄得臭气熏天。」
「不过『黄皮』这人不赖，」杰克说。「心眼儿实诚的好家伙。」
「实诚！」格兰特冷笑道。「男人混到让女人养活的地步，心就已经冷死了。那样活着的人，怎么还能在大庭广众之下招摇过市，倒不像条虫子似的钻进地底下藏起来？」他愤愤地转向雷。
===Original===
“Search me!” Ray laughed a little. “You might as well ask why all mulattoes have unpleasant voices.”
Grant was slightly embarrassed. He was yellow-skinned and his voice was hard and grainy. Jake he-hawed.
“Not all, chappie, I know some with sweet voice.”
“Mulattress, mon ami.” Ray lifted a finger. “That’s an exception. And now, James, let us forget Jake’s kind friend.”
===Chinese===
「这可把我问住了！」雷淡淡一笑。「你还不如去问，为什么黑白混血儿个个嗓音都不中听。」
格兰特微微一窘。他正是黄皮肤，嗓音又硬又糙。杰克呵呵地干笑起来。
「那也不尽然，老伙计，俺就认得几个嗓子甜的。」
「Mulattress（混血女郎），mon ami（我的朋友）。」雷竖起一根手指。「那是例外。好了，詹姆斯，咱们把杰克那位好心朋友撂开吧。」
===Original===
“Oh, I don’t mind him talking,” said Jake. “I don’t approve of Yaller’s trade mahself, but ef he can do it, well—It’s because you don’t know how many womens am running after the fellahs jest begging them to do that. They been after me moh time I can remember. There’s lots o’ folks living easy and living sweet, but…”
“There are as many forms of parasitism as there are ways of earning a living,” said Ray.
“But to live the life of carrion,” sneered Grant, “fatten on rotting human flesh. It’s the last ditch, where dogs go to die. When you drop down in that you cease being human.”
“You done said it straight out, brother,” said Jake. “It’s a stinking life and I don’t like stinks.”
“Your feeling against that sort of thing is fine, James,” said Ray. “But that’s the most I could say for it. It’s all right to start out with nice theories from an advantageous point in life. But when you get a chance to learn life for yourself, it’s quite another thing. The things you call fine human traits don’t belong to any special class or nation or race of people. Nobody can pull that kind of talk now and get away with it, least of all a Negro.”
===Chinese===
「嗨，他说他的，俺不恼，」杰克说。「『黄皮』那门营生，俺自己也瞧不上，可他既然干得来，那——那是因为你不晓得有多少娘们儿追在男人屁股后头，死乞白赖求人家干那号事儿。打俺记得起来的年头起，她们就追着俺不放。活得安逸、活得甜美的人有的是，可是……」
「谋生有多少路数，寄生就有多少形态，」雷说。
「可那是过腐尸的日子，」格兰特讥讽道。「靠腐烂的人肉养肥自己。那是最后一道壕沟，狗进去等死的地方。一个人跌到那一步，就不算是人了。」
「兄弟，你这话撂得实实在在，」杰克说。「那是条臭烘烘的日子，俺不喜欢臭味儿。」
「詹姆斯，你对那类事情怀着反感，这份心气儿是好的，」雷说。「可我顶多夸到这儿为止。从人生里一处占便宜的起点出发，揣着漂亮理论起步，那自然没什么。可等你有机会亲身去领教生活，就是另一回事了。你称作人类美德的那类东西，并不专属哪一个特殊的阶级、民族或种族。如今谁耍那套嘴皮子都糊弄不过去，黑人就尤其不行。」
===Original===
“Why not?” asked Grant. “Can’t a Negro have fine feelings about life?”
“Yes, but not the old false-fine feelings that used to be monopolized by educated and cultivated people. You should educate yourself away from that sort of thing.”
“But education is something to make you fine!”
“No, modern education is planned to make you a sharp, snouty, rooting hog. A Negro getting it is an anachronism. We ought to get something new, we Negroes. But we get our education like—like our houses. When the whites move out, we move in and take possession of the old dead stuff. Dead stuff that this age has no use for.”
===Chinese===
「为什么尤其不行？」格兰特问。「黑人就不能对生活怀有美好的感情么？」
「能。可不能是那种旧的假美好感情——从前叫有教育、有修养的人们垄断着的那种。你该受教育，好让自己离开那类东西。」
「可教育本来就是叫人变高雅的呀！」
「不，现代教育是成心要把你造成一头尖嘴、长鼻、拱土的肥猪。黑人受这种教育，是时代错位。我们黑人该弄点新东西才是。可我们得到的教育，就像——就像我们的房子。白人搬走，我们搬进去，接管那堆陈年的死物。死物——这个时代用不上的死物。」
===Original===
“How’s that?”
“Can you ask? You and I were born in the midst of the illness of this age and have lived through its agony.… Keep your fine feelings, indeed, but don’t try to make a virtue of them. You’ll lose them, then. They’ll become all hollow inside, false and dry as civilization itself. And civilization is rotten. We are all rotten who are touched by it.”
“I am not rotten,” retorted Grant, “and I couldn’t bring myself and my ideas down to the level of such filthy parasites.”
“All men have the disease of pimps in their hearts,” said Ray. “We can’t be civilized and not. I have seen your high and mighty civilized people do things that some pimps would be ashamed of—”
===Chinese===
「这话怎么讲？」
「这还用问？你我生在这个时代害病的当口，又一路熬过它的煎熬。……守住你那些美好感情，自然好，可千万别想把它们树成一种德行。那样你就丢了它们。它们会变得内里空空，虚假而干枯，跟文明本身一个成色。而文明是烂的。凡是叫它碰过的，我们都烂了。」
「我不烂，」格兰特回嘴道。「我也绝不肯把自己和自己的想法，降格到这等肮脏寄生虫的水平。」
「凡是男人，心里都带着吃软饭的病，」雷说。「又要文明，这病就去不掉。我见过你们那些高高在上、了不起的文明人，干出的事，连一些吃软饭的都要替他们害臊——」
===Original===
“You said it, then, and most truly,” cried Jake, who, lying on the bed, was intently following the dialogue.
“Do it in the name of civilization,” continued Ray. “And I have been forced down to the level of pimps and found some of them more than human. One of them was so strange.… I never thought he could feel anything. Never thought he could do what he did. Something so strange and wonderful and awful, it just lifted me up out of my little straight thoughts into a big whirl where all of life seemed hopelessly tangled and colored without point or purpose.”
“Tell us about it,” said Grant.
“All right,” said Ray. “I’ll tell it.”
===Chinese===
「你这话可说得千真万确，」杰克嚷道。他躺在床上，正一字不落地听着这场对话。
「干那些事，打的还是文明的旗号，」雷接着说。「我自己也被逼到过跟吃软饭的同一条水平线上，还发现他们当中有人，有超乎常人的地方。其中一个，就怪得出奇。……我从没想过他心里还有感觉。从没想过他能做出他做的那件事。那件事那样怪诞、那样奇妙、那样骇人，把人从窄窄直直的念头里一把拔出来，卷进一个大旋涡——在那里面，整个生活像一团永远解不开的乱麻，涂得五颜六色，没有头绪，也没有目的。」
「讲给我们听听，」格兰特说。
「好，」雷说。「我讲。」

## XVII He Also Loved / XVII 他也爱过

===Original===
It was in the winter of 1916 when I first came to New York to hunt for a job. I was broke. I was afraid I would have to pawn my clothes, and it was dreadfully cold. I didn’t even know the right way to go about looking for a job. I was always timid about that. For five weeks I had not paid my rent. I was worried, and Ma Lawton, my landlady, was also worried. She had her bills to meet. She was a good-hearted old woman from South Carolina. Her face was all wrinkled and sensitive like finely-carved mahogany.

Every bed-space in the flat was rented. I was living in the small hall bedroom. Ma Lawton asked me to give it up. There were four men sleeping in the front room; two in an old, chipped-enameled brass bed, one on a davenport, and the other in a folding chair. The old lady put a little canvas cot in that same room, gave me a pillow and a heavy quilt, and said I should try and make myself comfortable there until I got work.

The cot was all right for me. Although I hate to share a room with another person and the fellows snoring disturbed my rest. Ma Lawton moved into the little room that I had had, and rented out hers—it was next to the front room—to a man and a woman.

The woman was above ordinary height, chocolate-colored. Her skin was smooth, too smooth, as if it had been pressed and fashioned out for ready sale like chocolate candy. Her hair was straightened out into an Indian Straight after the present style among Negro ladies. She had a mongoose sort of a mouth, with two top front teeth showing. She wore a long mink coat.

===Chinese===
1916 年冬天，我头一回来纽约找事做。我囊空如洗，眼看连衣裳都怕要送进当铺，天气又冷得吓人。连找事该打哪儿下手，我都摸不着门路，我一向就是这份怯。房租已经五个礼拜没交了。我心里发愁，房东劳顿大妈（Ma Lawton）也发愁，她还有一堆账单要对付。她是南卡罗来纳来的老太太，心眼儿好。满脸的皱纹，又透着一种细腻的敏感，就像雕工极细的桃花心木。

公寓里每一张床位都租了出去。我原先住的是过厅旁的小间，劳顿大妈要我把它让出来。前屋睡四个男人：两个合睡一张掉了搪瓷的旧铜床，一个睡沙发床，还有一个睡折叠椅。老太太就在那间屋里支了一张小小的帆布行军床，给我一个枕头、一床厚被子，说我就凑合睡在那儿，找到事做再说。

行军床对我还算将就。只是我向来不爱跟别人共睡一间屋，那几位老兄的鼾声搅得我睡不安生。劳顿大妈搬进了我的小间，把她原来那间——就在前屋隔壁——租给了一男一女。

那女人个子比寻常人高，巧克力色。皮肤光滑，光滑得过了头，倒像是压模子做出来、专等着上柜的巧克力糖。头发照眼下黑人太太们时兴的样式拉直了，成一道印第安式直发。嘴有点像獴，两颗上门牙露在外头。身上裹一件长貂皮大衣。

===Original===
The man was darker than the woman. His face was longish, with the right cheek somewhat caved in. It was an interesting face, an attractive, salacious mouth, with the lower lip protruding. He wore a bottle-green peg-top suit, baggy at the hips. His coat hung loose from his shoulders and it was much longer than the prevailing style. He wore also a Mexican hat, and in his breast pocket he carried an Ingersoll watch attached to a heavy gold chain. His name was Jericho Jones, and they called him Jerco for short. And she was Miss Whicher—Rosalind Whicher.

Ma Lawton introduced me to them and said I was broke, and they were both awfully nice to me. They took me to a big feed of corned beef and cabbage at Burrell’s on Fifth Avenue. They gave me a good appetizing drink of gin to commence with. And we had beer with the eats; not ordinary beer, either, but real Budweiser, right off the ice.

And as good luck sometimes comes pouring down like a shower, the next day Ma Lawton got me a job in the little free-lunch saloon right under her flat. It wasn’t a paying job as far as money goes in New York, but I was glad to have it. I had charge of the free-lunch counter. You know the little dry crackers that go so well with beer, and the cheese and fish and the potato salad. And I served, besides, spareribs and whole boiled potatoes and corned beef and cabbage for those customers who could afford to pay for a lunch. I got no wages at all, but I got my eats twice a day. And I made a few tips, also. For there were about six big black men with plenty of money who used to eat lunch with us, specially for our spareribs and sweet potatoes. Each one of them gave me a quarter. I made enough to pay Ma Lawton for my canvas cot.

===Chinese===
那男人比女人黑。脸略长，右边腮帮子有点塌陷。是张有意思的脸：一张嘴巴又招人又带邪气，下唇微微外撅。他穿一身瓶绿色的萝卜裤套装，臀部空荡荡的。上衣松松垮垮地挂在肩上，比时兴的样式长出一大截。头上还扣一顶墨西哥帽，胸袋里揣着一块英格索尔表（Ingersoll），拴一条沉甸甸的金链子。他名叫杰里科·琼斯（Jericho Jones），简称杰科（Jerco）。她呢，是威彻小姐——罗莎琳德·威彻（Rosalind Whicher）。

劳顿大妈把我引荐给他们，说我正穷得叮当响，两人待我都好得很。他们带我上第五大道（Fifth Avenue）的伯雷尔（Burrell’s），大吃了一顿咸牛肉煮卷心菜。开席先请我灌下一盅开胃的好杜松子酒。吃菜还就着啤酒；可不是平常那种，是正牌百威（Budweiser），刚打冰里起出来的。

好运气有时候就像一阵骤雨，泼头浇下来。第二天，劳顿大妈就在她公寓正楼下的那家免费午餐小酒馆里，替我谋了份差事。按纽约的钱眼儿看，这活儿挣不来钱，可我已经求之不得。我管的是免费小吃柜台：就是那种就着啤酒最对味的干脆小饼干啦，奶酪啦，鱼啦，土豆沙拉啦。此外还给付得起饭钱的客人上猪肋排、整个儿的煮土豆、咸牛肉煮卷心菜。工钱是一个子儿也没有，可一天两顿饭有了着落，外带还能捞几个小费。有六七个手头阔绰的黑大汉，常来我们这儿吃午饭，专冲我们的猪肋排和红薯来。每位赏我两毛五。凑一凑，够付给劳顿大妈我那张行军床的钱了。

===Original===
Strange enough, too, Jerco and Rosalind took a liking to me. And sometimes they came and ate lunch perched up there at the counter, with Rosalind the only woman there, all made up and rubbing her mink coat against the men. And when they got through eating, Jerco would toss a dollar bill at me.

We got very friendly, we three. Rosalind would bring up squabs and canned stuff from the German delicatessen in 125th Street, and sometimes they asked me to dinner in their room and gave me good liquor.

I thought I was pretty well fixed for such a hard winter. All I had to do as extra work was keeping the saloon clean. … 

===Chinese===
说来也怪，杰科和罗莎琳德还挺喜欢我。有时他们来吃午饭，高高地坐在柜台边，罗莎琳德是满屋唯一的女人，浓妆艳抹，那件貂皮大衣在男人们身上蹭来蹭去。吃完了，杰科就朝我甩过来一张一块钱的票子。

我们三个人处得相当热络。罗莎琳德会从第 125 街的德国熟食店捎些嫩鸽和罐头上楼来，有时他们还请我上他们屋里吃晚饭，拿好酒款待我。

这样一个苦冬，我想着自己总算安顿得不坏了。额外的活儿，不过是把酒馆收拾干净。……

===Original===
One afternoon Jerco came into the saloon with a man who looked pretty near white. Of course, you never can tell for sure about a person’s race in Harlem, nowadays, when there are so many high-yallers floating round—colored folks that would make Italian and Spanish people look like Negroes beside them. But I figured out from his way of talking and acting that the man with Jerco belonged to the white race. They went in through the family entrance into the back room, which was unusual, for the family room of a saloon, as you know, is only for women in the business and the men they bring in there with them. Real men don’t sit in a saloon here as they do at home. I suppose it would be sissified. There’s a bar for them to lean on and drink and joke as long as they feel like.

The boss of the saloon was a little fidgety about Jerco and his friend sitting there in the back. The boss was a short pumpkin-bellied brown man, a little bald off the forehead. Twice he found something to attend to in the back room, although there was nothing at all there that wanted attending to. … I felt better, and the boss, too, I guess, when Rosalind came along and gave the family room its respectable American character. I served Rosalind a Martini cocktail extra dry, and afterward all three of them, Rosalind, Jerco, and their friend, went up to Ma Lawton’s.

The two fellows that slept together were elevator operators in a department store, so they had their Sundays free. On the afternoon of the Sunday of the same week that the white-looking man had been in the saloon with Jerco, I went upstairs to change my old shoes—they’d got soaking wet behind the counter—and I found Ma Lawton talking to the two elevator fellows.

===Chinese===
一天下午，杰科领着一个看上去几乎全白的男人进了酒馆。当然啦，如今在哈莱姆，单看外貌你根本断不定一个人的种族——满街晃荡的上等黄皮（high-yaller）太多了，那些有色人跟他们站在一起，倒把意大利人、西班牙人衬得像黑人了。不过从言谈举止看，我估摸跟杰科来的这位是白人。他们打家眷入口进去，进了后屋，这可不常见。要知道，酒馆的家眷间，向来只接待干那一行的女人和她们带来的男人。像样的男人在这儿可不兴像在自己家里那样坐着，我想那未免娘娘腔。酒吧台子有的是，靠在那儿，喝着，说着笑话，爱待多久待多久。

杰科和他朋友坐在后头，酒馆老板有点坐立不安。老板是个矮个子棕皮男人，南瓜似的肚皮，脑门儿有点秃。他两回找由头到后屋去张罗，其实那儿压根儿没有什么可张罗的。……等罗莎琳德一露面，给这间家眷间添上了体面的美国气派，我心里才踏实，我猜老板也一样。我给罗莎琳德伺候了一杯特干马天尼鸡尾酒，过后他们三位——罗莎琳德、杰科和他们的朋友——一道上劳顿大妈家去了。

合睡一张床的那两位是百货公司的电梯司机，礼拜天倒能歇着。就在那个白相男人跟杰科来过酒馆的同一个礼拜，礼拜天下午，我上楼去换双旧鞋——那双鞋在柜台后头已经浸得透湿——撞见劳顿大妈正跟那两个开电梯的说话。

===Original===
The boys had given Ma Lawton notice to quit. They said they couldn’t sleep there comfortably together on account of the goings-on in Rosalind’s room. The fellows were members of the Colored Y.M.C.A. and were queerly quiet and pious. One of them was studying to be a preacher. They were the sort of fellows that thought going to cabarets a sin, and that parlor socials were leading Harlem straight down to hell. They only went to church affairs. They had been rooming with Ma Lawton for over a year. She called them her gentlemen lodgers.

Ma Lawton said to me: “Have you heard anything phony outa the next room, dear?”

“Why, no, Ma,” I said, “nothing more unusual than you can hear all over Harlem. Besides, I work so late, I am dead tired when I turn in to bed, so I sleep heavy.”

“Well, it’s the truth I do like that there Jerco an’ Rosaline,” said Ma Lawton. “They did seem quiet as lambs, although they was always havin’ company. But Ise got to speak to them, ’cause I doana wanta lose ma young mens. … But theyse a real nice-acting couple. Jerco him treats me like him was mah son. It’s true that they doan work like all poah niggers, but they pays that rent down good and prompt ehvery week.”

===Chinese===
那两个小伙子已经给劳顿大妈打了退租的招呼。说是罗莎琳德屋里那档子事闹得他们俩睡不踏实。这二位是有色基督教青年会（Colored Y.M.C.A.）的会员，出奇地安分、虔诚。有一个还在念书预备当传道士。他们是那种把逛歌舞厅看作罪过、认为客厅社交会正把哈莱姆笔直引向地狱的人。他们只参加教会活动。他们在劳顿大妈这儿住了一年多，大妈管他们叫她的绅士房客。

劳顿大妈对我说：「乖，隔壁屋里的动静，你听见什么不对味的没有？」

「嗐，没有哇，大妈，」我说，「顶多是全哈莱姆哪儿都听得见的那点事儿。再说我收工晚，挨到床上已经累得半死，一觉睡得死沉。」

「唉，说真的，那个杰科跟罗莎琳，俺是真心疼他们，」劳顿大妈说。「他们瞧着也真跟羊羔似的安生，虽说家里老是宾客不断。可俺不能不去跟他们说道说道，俺不能丢了俺这两个年轻房客。……不过他们两口子行事是真体面。杰科他待俺，就跟俺亲儿子似的。不错，他们是不像那些穷苦黑鬼似的卖力气干活，可人家房租给得又痛快又准，个礼拜都不落。」

===Original===
Jerco was always bringing in ice-cream and cake or something for Ma Lawton. He had a way about him, and everybody liked him. He was a sympathetic type. He helped Ma Lawton move beds and commodes and he fixed her clothes lines. I had heard somebody talking about Jerco in the saloon, however, saying that he could swing a mean fist when he got his dander up, and that he had been mixed up in more than one razor cut-up. He did have a nasty long razor scar on the back of his right hand.

The elevator fellows had never liked Rosalind and Jerco. The one who was studying to preach Jesus said he felt pretty sure that they were an ungodly-living couple. He said that late one night he had pointed out their room to a woman that looked white. He said the woman looked suspicious. She was perfumed and all powdered up and it appeared as if she didn’t belong among colored people.

“There’s no sure telling white from high-yaller these days,” I said. “There are so many swell-looking quadroons and octoroons of the race.”

But the other elevator fellow said that one day in the tenderloin section he had run up against Rosalind and Jerco together with a petty officer of marines. And that just put the lid on anything favorable that could be said about them.

===Chinese===
杰科老是给劳顿大妈捎冰激凌、蛋糕什么的来。他这人自有讨人喜欢的一套，谁见了都喜欢。他是有同情心的那一路人。帮大妈挪床挪柜，替她拾掇晾衣绳。不过在酒馆里，我也听人议论过杰科，说他火气一上来，抡起拳头又狠又毒，还说他卷进过的剃刀斗殴不止一回。他右手背上确确实实有一道长长的剃刀疤，瞧着狰狞。

那两个开电梯的从来没喜欢过罗莎琳德和杰科。预备传耶稣之道的那个说，他十分有把握，这一对儿过得是不敬神的日子。他说有一天深夜，他给一个看上去像白人的女人指过他们那个房间。他说那女人看着就蹊跷：浑身香水，粉拍得白白净净，简直不像有色人堆里的人。

「如今白皮跟上等黄皮，你根本没法拿准，」我说。「咱们族里那些气派好看的四分之一混血、八分之一混血，多得是呢。」

可另一个开电梯的说，有一天在城里的嫩腰区（Tenderloin），他迎面撞见罗莎琳德和杰科跟一个海军陆战队军士走在一块。这一来，再想替他们说句好话，是全然不能了。

===Original===
But Ma Lawton said: “Well, Ise got to run mah flat right an’ try mah utmost to please youall, but I ain’t wanta dip mah nose too deep in a lodger’s affairs.”

Late that night, toward one o’clock, Jerco dropped in at the saloon and told me that Rosalind was feeling badly. She hadn’t eaten a bite all day and he had come to get a pail of beer, because she had asked specially for draught beer. Jerco was worried, too.

“I hopes she don’t get bad,” he said. “For we ain’t got a cent o’ money. Wese just in on a streak o’ bad luck.”

“I guess she’ll soon be all right,” I said.

===Chinese===
可劳顿大妈说：「唉，俺总得把俺这公寓管得正正的，想法子叫你们大伙儿都称心，可房客自家的事儿，俺不乐意把鼻子伸得太深。」

那天深夜，将近一点钟，杰科晃进酒馆，说罗莎琳德身上不爽利。一整天没沾一粒米，他这是来打一桶啤酒的，因为她点名要生啤酒。杰科自己也愁眉不展。

「俺就怕她的病见沉，」他说。「俺们身上一个子儿也没有了。俺们正摊上一截子霉运呢。」

「我看她很快就会好的，」我说。

===Original===
The next day after lunch I stole a little time and went up to see Rosalind. Ma Lawton was just going to attend to her when I let myself in, and she said to me: “Now the poor woman is sick, poor chile, ahm so glad mah conscience is free and that I hadn’t a said nothing evil t’ her.”

Rosalind was pretty sick. Ma Lawton said it was the grippe. She gave Rosalind hot whisky drinks and hot milk, and she kept her feet warm with a hot-water bottle. Rosalind’s legs were lead-heavy. She had a pain that pinched her side like a pair of pincers. And she cried out for thirst and begged for draught beer.

Ma Lawton said Rosalind ought to have a doctor. “You’d better go an’ scares up a white one,” she said to Jerco. “Ise nevah had no faith in these heah nigger doctors.”

“I don’t know how we’ll make out without money,” Jerco whined. He was sitting in the old Morris chair with his head heavy on his left hand.

===Chinese===
第二天午饭后，我偷了个空儿上楼去看罗莎琳德。我推门进去时，劳顿大妈正要去照应她。大妈对我说：「瞧瞧，这可怜的女人病倒了，可怜的孩子，俺心里真高兴，俺的良心干干净净，没跟她说过一句恶话。」

罗莎琳德病得不轻。劳顿大妈说是流行性感冒。她给罗莎琳德灌热威士忌、热牛奶，拿热水袋焐着她的脚。罗莎琳德的两条腿沉得像灌了铅。肋边一阵疼，像一把钳子夹着。她渴得直叫唤，一口一声讨生啤酒喝。

劳顿大妈说罗莎琳德该请个医生。「你顶好去赶紧轰一个白人大夫来，」她对杰科说。「这儿的黑人大夫，俺从来信不过。」

「没有钱，俺不知道可怎么对付得过去，」杰科哀声抱怨。他坐在那把旧莫里斯椅（Morris chair）里，脑袋沉沉地支在左手上。

===Original===
“You kain pawn my coat,” said Rosalind. “Old man Greenbaum will give you two hundred down without looking at it.”

“I won’t put a handk’chief o’ yourn in the hock shop,” said Jerco. “You’ll need you’ stuff soon as you get better. Specially you’ coat. You kain’t go anywheres without it.”

“S’posin’ I don’t get up again,” Rosalind smiled. But her countenance changed suddenly as she held her side and moaned. Ma Lawton bent over and adjusted the pillows.

Jerco pawned his watch chain and his own overcoat, and called in a Jewish doctor from the upper Eighth Avenue fringe of the Belt. But Rosalind did not improve under medical treatment. She lay there with a sad, tired look, as if she didn’t really care what happened to her. Her lower limbs were apparently paralyzed. Jerco told the doctor that she had been sick unto death like that before. The doctor shot a lot of stuff into her system. But Rosalind lay there heavy and fading like a felled tree.

===Chinese===
「我的大衣你可不能当，」罗莎琳德说。「格林鲍姆（Greenbaum）老头连看都不看，当场就会点给你两百块。」

「你的一片手绢儿俺也不会送进当铺，」杰科说。「你好起来，这些行头样样都用得上。顶要紧是你那件大衣。没有它你哪儿也去不成。」

「万一俺再也起不来呢，」罗莎琳德笑道。可她忽然脸色一变，捂住肋边呻吟起来。劳顿大妈俯下身去，把枕头掖好。

杰科当掉了他的表链和自己那件大衣，又从黑人区边上、第八大道上游那一带请来一位犹太医生。可医治之下，罗莎琳德不见起色。她躺在那儿，一脸忧伤的倦容，仿佛自己落到什么地步全不在乎。两条下腿分明已经瘫了。杰科告诉医生，她从前也这样病得奄奄一息过。医生往她身体里注射了一大堆药水。可罗莎琳德躺在那儿，沉沉的，像一棵放倒的树，一点点枯萎下去。

===Original===
The elevator operators looked in on her. The student one gave her a Bible with a little red ribbon marking the chapter in St. John’s Gospel about the woman taken in adultery.

He also wanted to pray for her recovery. Jerco wanted the prayer, but Rosalind said no. Her refusal shocked Ma Lawton, who believed in God’s word.

The doctor stopped Rosalind from drinking beer. But Jerco slipped it in to her when Ma Lawton was not around. He said he couldn’t refuse it to her when beer was the only thing she cared for. He had an expensive sweater. He pawned it. He also pawned their large suitcase. It was real leather and worth a bit of money.

===Chinese===
那两个开电梯的来看过她。预备当传道士的那个送她一部《圣经》，用一根小红丝带夹在《约翰福音》记行淫被拿的妇人那一章。

他还想为她康复祷告。杰科愿意领受祷告，罗莎琳德却拒绝了。这一拒绝叫劳顿大妈大为震惊，大妈是信上帝的话语的。

医生不许罗莎琳德再喝啤酒。可趁劳顿大妈不在跟前，杰科偷偷给她递。他说啤酒是她唯一惦记的东西，他不忍心不给。他有一件贵重的毛衣，当掉了。连他们那只大皮箱也当了。真皮的，值几个钱。

===Original===
One afternoon Jerco sat alone in the back room of the saloon and began to cry.

“I’d do anything. There ain’t anything too low I wouldn’t do to raise a little money,” he said.

“Why don’t you hock Rosalind’s fur coat?” I suggested. “That’ll give you enough money for a while.”

“Gawd, no! I wouldn’t touch none o’ Rosalind’s clothes. I jest kain’t,” he said. “She’ll need them as soon as she’s better.”

“Well, you might try and find some sort of a job, then,” I said.

===Chinese===
一天下午，杰科独自坐在酒馆后屋里，哭了起来。

「俺什么都肯干。只要能凑几个钱，再下作的事俺也干得出来，」他说。

「你怎么不当了罗莎琳德的皮大衣？」我出主意。「那够你们对付一阵子的。」

「老天爷，那可不行！罗莎琳德的衣裳，俺一件也不碰，俺就是不能，」他说。「她一好起来就要用的。」

「那你去寻个什么活儿干干看也好啊，」我说。

===Original===
“Me find a job? What kain I do? I ain’t no good foh no job. I kain’t work. I don’t know how to ask for no job. I wouldn’t know how. I wish I was a woman.”

“Good God! Jerco,” I said, “I don’t see any way out for you but some sort of a job.”

“What kain I do? What kain I do?” he whined. “I kain’t do nothing. That’s why I don’t wanta hock Rosalind’s fur coat. She’ll need it soon as she’s better. Rosalind’s so wise about picking up good money. Just like that!” He snapped his fingers.

I left Jerco sitting there and went into the saloon to serve a customer a plate of corned beef and cabbage.

===Chinese===
「俺找活儿？俺能干得了啥？什么活儿俺都干不来。俺干不了活。怎么开口去找活儿，俺不知道。压根儿不会。俺恨不得自己是个女人家。」

「我的老天！杰科，」我说，「依我看，除了找份活儿干，你没有别的路可走。」

「俺能干啥？俺能干啥？」他哀哀地哼。「俺啥也干不来。就是因为这个，俺才不肯当罗莎琳德的皮大衣。她一好起来就要用的。弄大钱，罗莎琳德精着呢。喏，就这么一下子！」他把手指捻得吧嗒响。

我撂下杰科叫他坐在那儿，进店堂给一位客人端咸牛肉煮卷心菜去了。

===Original===
After lunch I thought I’d go up to see how Rosalind was making out. The door was slightly open, so I slipped in without knocking. I saw Jerco kneeling down by the open wardrobe and kissing the toe of one of her brown shoes. He started as he saw me, and looked queer kneeling there. It was a high old-fashioned wardrobe that Ma Lawton must have picked up at some sale. Rosalind’s coat was hanging there, and it gave me a spooky feeling, for it looked so much more like the real Rosalind than the woman that was dozing there on the bed.

Her other clothes were hanging there, too. There were three gowns—a black silk, a glossy green satin, and a flimsy chiffon-like yellow thing. In a corner of the lowest shelf was a bundle of soiled champagne-colored silk stockings and in the other four pairs of shoes—one black velvet, one white kid, and another gold-finished. Jerco regarded the lot with doglike affection.

“I wouldn’t touch not one of her things until she’s better,” he said. “I’d sooner hock the shirt off mah back.”

===Chinese===
午饭过后，我想上楼去看看罗莎琳德怎么样了。门虚掩着，我就没敲门，溜了进去。只见杰科跪在敞开的大衣柜跟前，亲她一只棕鞋的鞋尖。他看见我，吓了一跳，跪在那儿一副怪模样。那是口高大的老式衣柜，准是劳顿大妈打哪场拍卖上淘来的。罗莎琳德的大衣就挂在里面，这叫我心里发瘆，因为它看上去远比床上那个打盹的女人更像真的罗莎琳德。

她其余的衣裳也全挂在里面。三条长裙——一条黑绸的，一条油亮的绿缎子，还有一条薄薄的、雪纺纱似的黄玩意儿。最底下一层搁板的角上，团着一卷穿脏了的香槟色丝袜；另一角是四双鞋——一双黑绒面，一双白小羊皮，还有一双金光闪闪的。杰科满眼狗一样依恋的深情，望着这一堆东西。

「她不好起来，她的东西俺一件也不动，」他说。「俺宁可把俺背上的衬衫剥下来去当。」

===Original===
Which he was preparing to do. He had three expensive striped silk shirts, presents from Rosalind. He had just taken two out of the wardrobe and the other off his back, and made a parcel of them for old Greenbaum. … Rosalind woke up and murmured that she wanted some beer. …

A little later Jerco came to the saloon with the pail. He was shivering. His coat collar was turned up and fastened with a safety pin, for he only had an undershirt on.

“I don’t know what I’d do if anything happens to Rosalind,” he said. “I kain’t live without her.”

“Oh yes, you can,” I said in a not very sympathetic tone. Jerco gave me such a reproachful pathetic look that I was sorry I said it.

===Chinese===
他也真就要这么办。他有三件值钱的条纹绸衬衫，都是罗莎琳德送的。他刚从衣柜里取出两件，又脱下身上的那一件，打成一个包裹，预备送去给老格林鲍姆。……罗莎琳德醒来，咕哝着说她要喝点啤酒。……

过了一小会儿，杰科拎着桶来到酒馆。他浑身打颤。大衣领子竖起来，拿一枚安全别针别着，因为他里面只穿了一件汗衫。

「罗莎琳德要是有个三长两短，俺真不知道该怎么办，」他说。「没有她，俺活不成。」

「哦，不，你活得成，」我说，话音里没什么同情。杰科看了我一眼，那眼色又怨又惨，倒叫我又后悔说了这话。

===Original===
The tall big fellow had turned into a scared, trembling baby. “You ought to buck up and hold yourself together,” I told him. “Why, you ought to be game if you like Rosalind, and don’t let her know you’re down in the dumps.”

“I’ll try,” he said. “She don’t know how miserable I am. When I hooks up with a woman I treat her right, but I never let her know everything about me. Rosalind is an awful good woman. The straightest woman I ever had, honest.”

I gave him a big glass of strong whisky.

Ma Lawton came in the saloon about nine o’clock that evening and said that Rosalind was dead. “I told Jerco we’d have to sell that theah coat to give the poah woman a decent fun’ral, an’ he jest brokes down crying like a baby.”

===Chinese===
这么个高高大大的汉子，竟变成了一个受了惊、直哆嗦的娃娃。「你该振作起来，撑住自己，」我对他说。「嗨，你要是喜欢罗莎琳德，就该拿出点勇气来，别叫她知道你灰心丧气。」

「俺试着来，」他说。「俺心里多苦，她不知道。俺跟哪个女人搭伙，都待她不薄，可俺从来不叫她把俺知道个底儿掉。罗莎琳德是个顶好的女人。俺有过的女人里头，她最正经，真的。」

我给他斟了满满一大杯烈威士忌。

那天晚上九点来钟，劳顿大妈进酒馆来说，罗莎琳德死了。「俺跟杰科说，咱们得把那件大衣卖了，好给这可怜的女人办个像样的丧事，他一下子就垮了，哭得跟个娃娃似的。」

===Original===
That night Ma Lawton slept in the kitchen and put Jerco in her little hall bedroom. He was all broken up. I took him up a pint of whisky.

“I’ll nevah find another one like Rosalind,” he said, “nevah!” He sat on an old black-framed chair in which a new yellow-varnished bottom had just been put.
